2026-11-25

Title: Validation Bias in Large Language Model Responses to Cancer Treatment Queries: A Factorial Cross-Sectional Study

  • Authors: Ramez Kouzy, Saketh Vinjamuri, Sarah Ghalayini, Jack Gallifant, Shan Chen, Marielle Fis Loperana, Roxanna Attar-Olyaee, Danielle S. Bitterman
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31251
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31251
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31251]] Validation Bias in Large Language Model Responses to Cancer Treatment Queries: A Factorial Cross-Sectional Study(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31251)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language model (LLM) chatbots are increasingly used by patients seeking cancer information, but their safety when users present evidence-misaligned beliefs about treatment remains uncharacterized. We hypothesized that LLMs exhibit validation bias, affirming rather than correcting such beliefs, and evaluated this with a 2x2x2 factorial design crossing conversation format (single-turn vs multi-turn), treatment type (evidence-based vs alternative), and query valence (positive vs negative). Framing was evidence-aligned (supporting evidence-based treatments or questioning alternative therapies) or evidence-misaligned (promoting alternative treatments or questioning evidence-based therapies). We analyzed 1,272 conversations (6,360 interactions) across 14 LLMs from 6 vendors, scored by an LLM judge (GPT-5-mini) validated against human annotation on a 6-dimension safety rubric. The primary outcome was the unsafe response rate, defined as a Weak classification or any critical failure. Evidence-misaligned framing increased unsafe response rates compared with evidence-aligned framing (14.4% vs 5.1%; OR 3.14, 95% CI 2.07-4.78; P < .001), and the association persisted after accounting for clustering by LLM. Among 429 conversations classified as Strong under evidence-aligned framing, 22.6% were downgraded in their paired evidence-misaligned counterpart. Multi-turn conversations had lower unsafe response rates than single-turn conversations (5.2% vs 14.7%; P < .001). Unsafe response rates varied by vendor from 1.8% to 36.8%. LLMs exhibited significant validation bias in oncology, with unsafe responses nearly tripling under evidence-misaligned framing. Evaluations limited to neutral, fact-seeking queries may overestimate real-world safety, and safety testing of medical AI systems should include evidence-misaligned user framing.

Title: DAGGER: Deployment-Free Quality Estimation for Generative AI Workflows

  • Authors: Dimitrios Liakopoulos, Neeraja J. Yadwadkar
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31252
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31252
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31252]] DAGGER: Deployment-Free Quality Estimation for Generative AI Workflows(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31252)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Estimating the quality of generative AI workflows is challenging because these workflows comprise multiple stages, and errors can propagate across them, making end-to-end performance depend not only on the accuracy of individual models but also on how their outputs affect downstream stages. Each stage can use one of many candidate models. Estimating end-to-end workflow quality by deploying and evaluating every resulting model configuration is therefore prohibitively expensive. As a result, existing approaches face a tradeoff between estimation cost and accuracy: inexpensive methods often produce unreliable quality estimates, while accurate methods require deploying and evaluating many workflow configurations. In this paper, we introduce DAGGER, a framework for estimating the quality of generative AI workflows without exhaustive deployment and profiling. DAGGER represents a workflow as a graph, in which nodes correspond to stages and edges capture how outputs from one stage affect subsequent stages. It then decomposes workflow quality into per-node accuracy terms and per-edge dependency terms. Using a novel analytical formulation and SVD-based matrix completion, DAGGER estimates workflow quality from sparse observations without executing every candidate workflow. We evaluate DAGGER on three generative AI workflows across three datasets. DAGGER estimates workflow quality with a median error of 6.8 percentage points while reducing profiling cost by 10.2$\times$ relative to BenchPress, a state-of-the-art baseline, and selects workflow configurations competitive with state-of-the-art frameworks such as LLMSelector.

Title: Directly Maximizing On-policy Expected Accepted Length for Speculative Decoding

  • Authors: Ziying Zhang, Yaqing Wang, Yong Liu, Quanming Yao
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31256
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31256
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31256]] Directly Maximizing On-policy Expected Accepted Length for Speculative Decoding(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31256)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates large language model inference by using a lightweight draft model to propose tokens for verification by a target model. Draft training commonly optimizes token-level distribution alignment, but this does not directly optimize the length of the accepted prefix: once a draft token is rejected, all subsequent draft tokens are discarded. We propose FreDraft, an on-policy training method that directly maximizes the expected accepted length of drafts sampled from the current draft model. This objective accounts for first-rejection truncation and induces prefix-aware credit assignment, weighting each token by its contribution to the acceptance of the current and subsequent tokens. To optimize the objective efficiently, we analytically marginalize token-level acceptance rate, using the target model's vocabulary distribution to reduce gradient variance. We further redistribute part of this distribution-level gradient into an explicit penalty for sampled tokens whose probabilities are overestimated by the draft model, while preserving the expected gradient. Experiments across multiple benchmarks and target models show that FreDraft improves accepted length and decoding speedup over strong baselines, achieving faster on-policy training convergence.

Title: scOPD: Routed On-Policy Self-Distillation for LLM-Based Single-Cell Annotation

  • Authors: Zhiyin Yu, Yuchen Mou, Songtao Huang, Shuaiyu Zhang, Xing Wei, Chunchun Chen, Wei Ye, Xiao Luo, Zhonghai Wu, Xiangchao Yan, Lei Bai, Shiyang Feng, Bo Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31257
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31257
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31257]] scOPD: Routed On-Policy Self-Distillation for LLM-Based Single-Cell Annotation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31257)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Motivation: Large language models (LLMs) have shown promise for single-cell annotation, but existing training methods mainly rely on scalar outcome rewards that provide limited supervision for intermediate biological reasoning. Batch-level annotation is particularly challenging because it requires jointly resolving local cell identities and a global one-to-one matching constraint, such that one local error can affect the entire assignment. Moreover, a single inference pass provides insufficient evidence for locating uncertain predictions. These limitations call for fine-grained reasoning supervision during training and uncertainty-aware refinement during inference. Results: To address these challenges, we propose scOPD, an on-policy self-distillation framework for LLM-based single-cell annotation. After reasoning fine-tuning, scOPD adaptively routes qualified rollouts to reward-based optimization, while erroneous rollouts receive privileged token-level supervision from successful sibling trajectories. During inference, rollout disagreement triggers constraint-aware pairwise refinement of uncertain assignments. Across CellPuzzles and four out-of-domain disease datasets, scOPD consistently improves the performance of diverse LLM backbones, achieving average cell-level and batch-level accuracies of 81.71% and 44.49% with Qwen3-8B. Availability and Implementation: Code and data are available at this https URL.

Title: RECAP: Training Long-horizon LLM Agents with Context Compaction

  • Authors: Yulun Jiang, Maria Brbic, Michael Moor
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31258
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31258
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31258]] RECAP: Training Long-horizon LLM Agents with Context Compaction(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31258)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Context compaction enables large language model agents to tackle long-horizon tasks within limited context budgets by replacing interaction histories with compressed summaries. However, learning effective compaction jointly with the agent policy remains challenging, as summaries affect task success over many subsequent interactions. We introduce RECAP, a reinforcement learning (RL) framework based on the principle that a summary should preserve the behavior induced by the original interaction history. We formalize this principle with a behavior distortion objective that measures the divergence between the agent's subsequent behavior under compressed and original contexts. We show that the gradient of the distortion objective decomposes into two complementary learning signals: distortion-based reward for summary generation and self-distillation for subsequent actions. Experiments on Qwen3.5-4B show that RECAP consistently outperforms the task-reward-only RL baselines on both environments, leading to an absolute improvement of 4.6% and 4.3% on BrowseComp-plus and SWE-bench Verified, respectively. Ablations further highlight the value of jointly supervising summary generation and post-compaction behavior for RECAP.

Title: Capability and Interpretability of Transformers on Multi-task Recurrence

  • Authors: Xiamin Chen, Linyu Liu, Jiachen Hu, Pinyan Lu
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31260
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31260
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31260]] Capability and Interpretability of Transformers on Multi-task Recurrence(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31260)
  • Keywords: interpretability, transformer
  • Abstract: Transformers are widely used in various language tasks, with limited understanding of their internal mechanisms. Motivated by the recurrent structure of many linguistic rules, we study the capability and interpretability of Transformers through a controlled family of linear recurrence tasks over finite cyclic groups. These tasks range from executing a single fixed rule to identifying a latent rule from context and switching between explicitly specified rules, with and without input masking. Through extensive experiments across different configurations, we find that switching specified rules is simpler than identifying latent rules for Transformers in general. We also identify how several key factors affect the generalization capability of Transformers, including the depth, width, and the algebraic structure of the linear recurrence. Curriculum training is extremely useful when the number of rules is large. With standard tools in mechanistic interpretability like linear probing and input patching, our study reveals how Transformers represent, identify, and execute multiple rules.

Title: RegioZH: Unveiling Comprehension Disparities across Regional Chinese Varieties in LLMs

  • Authors: Weihua Zheng, Wu Kui, Thong T. Doan, Lin Jingxia, AiTi Aw, Nancy F. Chen, Roy Ka-Wei Lee
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31262
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31262
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31262]] RegioZH: Unveiling Comprehension Disparities across Regional Chinese Varieties in LLMs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31262)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Current evaluations often treat Chinese varieties as monolithic, obscuring failures for regional users. We introduce RegioZH, a 2,607-item multiple-choice benchmark for regional lexical reasoning across Mainland China, Taiwan, Hong Kong, Singapore, and Malaysia. Built by region-specific annotators and expert-validated, it targets localized idioms, slang, code-mixing, politeness conventions, and sociocultural expressions. Zero-shot results on eleven LLMs show large disparities, including a 29.4-point average accuracy gap between Taiwan and Singapore. Persona and structured chain-of-thought prompts for five models yield selective gains, suggesting that some errors stem from retrieval or framing rather than only missing knowledge. Representation, attention, calibration, and surface-feature analyses show a dissociation between marker attention and semantic grounding, overconfident forced-choice errors, and localized lexical density as a consistent difficulty predictor. Qualitative analysis finds terminology misunderstandings, sociocultural inference gaps, and cross-regional semantic divergence. RegioZH demonstrates that apparent Chinese proficiency can mask uneven understanding across regional varieties, motivating intra-language robustness evaluation alongside multilingual generalization.

Title: Evaluating Large Language Models on Iranian Doctoral Entrance Exams in Electrical and Computer Engineering

  • Authors: Sima Akbari Neisiyani, Hossein Taghizad
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31263
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31263
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31263]] Evaluating Large Language Models on Iranian Doctoral Entrance Exams in Electrical and Computer Engineering(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31263)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: We evaluate four large language models (LLMs) on multiple-choice questions from the 2024 and 2025 Iranian national Ph.D. entrance examinations in Electrical Engineering (EE) and Computer Engineering (CE). Of the 440 questions in the four official booklets, 7 were excluded because the question statement or its figure was ambiguous, leaving 433 questions (186 EE, 247 CE), of which 171 (39.5\%) depend on a figure. Each question was presented in the original Persian and in an English translation, giving 3,464 scored responses. GPT-5, Gemini 2.5 Pro, Claude Sonnet 4.5 and Grok 4 were queried zero-shot, with reasoning enabled, through a single commercial web interface (Perplexity) in October 2025, using an automated script. Because every model answers the same items, all inferences use paired tests or question-clustered models. Accuracy separates into two tiers: GPT-5 (79.9\%, 95\% CI 76.6--83.1) and Gemini 2.5 Pro (78.4\%, 74.8--81.9) are statistically indistinguishable, and both outperform Grok 4 (71.7\%) and Claude Sonnet 4.5 (71.3\%) by 6.7--8.7 percentage points (Holm-adjusted $p < 0.001$). The English--Persian accuracy difference is small for every model ($-0.2$ to $+2.5$ points; exact McNemar $p \ge 0.28$). For three models the 90\% confidence interval lies within $\pm 5$ points, so differences larger than this can be excluded. Questions with figures are much harder: the odds ratio is 0.30 (95\% CI 0.21--0.43) after adjusting for discipline. The apparent EE deficit of two models disappears once the larger share of figure-based EE items is accounted for. We describe the dataset, the protocol and its limitations, including the uncontrolled web-retrieval and decoding settings of the interface, and release item-level responses and analysis code.

Title: How Persona Conditioning Changes Open-Ended Task Execution: A Paired Evaluation of Behavioral Gains and Reliability Costs

  • Authors: Yugyeong Ji, Changho Lee, Yerin Hwang, Taegwan Kang, Jinsik Lee
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31264
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31264
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31264]] How Persona Conditioning Changes Open-Ended Task Execution: A Paired Evaluation of Behavioral Gains and Reliability Costs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31264)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are often prompted with role- or persona-based instructions, yet persona conditioning has mainly been evaluated through character fidelity or aggregate benchmark accuracy. We study persona conditioning as a task-execution steering signal that changes how models interpret, structure, and sustain responses. Using paired instances derived from real user role-play logs and expanded into simulated multi-turn continuations, we compare with-persona and no-persona conditions while fixing task content, constraints, user simulation, and evaluation criteria. Across eight models, persona conditioning improves expert content depth and procedural fidelity, and these gains survive length control. At the same time, it lowers strict instruction-following accuracy by 17 to 30 points in every model evaluated, and increases unsupported real-world specificity through persona-induced elaboration. These results suggest that persona prompting is not a general-purpose performance improvement technique, but a selective task-execution policy shift with both behavioral gains and reliability risks.

Title: SD-Cache: Learnable Memory Consolidation with State-Guided Dynamic Caching

  • Authors: Yuanhong Chen, Yiping Ji, Cameron Gordon, Emily Hackett-Jones, Hemanth Saratchandran, Anton van den Hengel
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31265
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31265
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31265]] SD-Cache: Learnable Memory Consolidation with State-Guided Dynamic Caching(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31265)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as persistent services that must reason over large volumes of external knowledge. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) grounds model responses in relevant documents, but the retrieved content must still be encoded and stored in the key-value (KV) cache. As this content accumulates, the cache grows linearly, increasing memory consumption and inference cost. Existing memory-compression methods address this problem either by retaining a subset of cached states or by learning context compressors that map documents to compact latent representations. Despite these advances, consolidating information under tight memory budgets while preserving its usefulness for future queries remains challenging. We investigate how the model's native KV states can be selectively consolidated into a fixed-size working memory without access to future queries. To this end, we introduce SD-Cache (State-guided and Dynamically updated Cache), a query-agnostic KV-memory consolidation framework that operates alongside a frozen generator LLM. SD-Cache learns to consolidate layer-wise KV states into fixed-size memory through selective anchor-based pooling and state-guided refinement, processing new contexts in a single forward pass without context-specific optimisation. Experiments on retrieval-augmented question answering and synthetic long-context benchmarks show that SD-Cache maintains competitive downstream performance under constrained memory budgets.

Title: AhaPro: A Process-level Rebus benchmark for probing Aha moments in LLM reasoning

  • Authors: Wei Guo, Lang Qin, Yundi Gai, Qinghua Xu, Zhi Wu, Wenjian Ding, Huijia Li, Zhenglu Yang, Yao Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31268
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31268
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31268]] AhaPro: A Process-level Rebus benchmark for probing Aha moments in LLM reasoning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31268)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Existing research on Aha moments in large language models has largely focused on reflective expressions or final revision outcomes, leaving their role in the visible reasoning process insufficiently characterized. Rebus puzzles encode answers through character forms, spatial arrangements, and homophony. Reinterpreting key details may trigger Aha moments and shifts in the reasoning process and candidate answers. We introduce AhaPro, a process-level benchmark that uses rebus puzzles to examine how models reorganize evidence and revise answers through Aha moments. The framework represents CoT as sequences of candidate answers, evidence, and verification states, using hierarchical metrics to assess conflict detection, state improvement, and subsequent adoption. Across eleven models, we observe frequent failures to adopt better states discovered during Aha moments, alongside adopted revisions that leave state quality unchanged or reduce it. Models also detect conflicts without subsequently achieving an effective revision. Further paired interventions suggest that prior reasoning context may hinder effective revision after conflict detection, while multi-branch search highlights answer selection as a bottleneck between correct-answer discovery and successful correction.

Title: Chain-of-Thought Is Not Explainability

  • Authors: Fazl Barez, Tung-Yu Wu, Iván Arcuschin, Michael Lan, Vincent Wang, Noah Siegel, Nicolas Collignon, Clement Neo, Isabelle Lee, Alasdair Paren, John Yan, Adel Bibi, Damiano Fornasiere, Robert Trager, Yanai Elazar, Yoshua Bengio
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31271
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31271
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31271]] Chain-of-Thought Is Not Explainability(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31271)
  • Keywords: interpretability, explainability
  • Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) allows language models to verbalise multi-step rationales before producing their final answer. While this technique often boosts task performance and offers an impression of transparency into the model's reasoning, this position paper argues that rationales generated by current CoT techniques can be misleading and are neither necessary nor sufficient for trustworthy interpretability. We analyse faithfulness as whether CoTs are not only human-interpretable but also reflect the model's underlying reasoning well enough to support responsible use. By synthesising evidence from prior work, we systematically show that verbalised chains are frequently unfaithful, diverging from the critical hidden computations that drive a model's predictions. As CoT is increasingly relied upon in application domains, including high-stakes ones such as medicine, law, and autonomous systems, we further argue that this unfaithfulness is not taken seriously enough in these settings: our analysis of 1,000 recent CoT-centric papers finds that approximately 21.7% explicitly treat CoT as a load-bearing interpretability technique, building pipelines or safety arguments that would not hold if CoT is unfaithful to the model's actual reasoning. They include papers in high-stakes domains that heavily hinge on such interpretability claims. Building on prior work, we make three proposals: (i) avoid treating CoT as being sufficient for interpretability without additional verification, (ii) adopt rigorous methods that assess faithfulness for downstream decision-making, and (iii) report such assessments alongside any interpretability claims based on CoT, while continuing to use CoT for its communicative and monitoring benefits.

Title: Information Security Risk Preparedness in Electronic Data Interchange: A Comparative Assessment of General IT and Healthcare Environments

  • Authors: Naresh Kshetri, Bishwo Prakash Pokharel, Arati Bastakoti, Anjana Bastakoti
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31294
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31294
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31294]] Information Security Risk Preparedness in Electronic Data Interchange: A Comparative Assessment of General IT and Healthcare Environments(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31294)
  • Keywords: security, protect
  • Abstract: Electronic Data Interchange (EDI) supports the exchange of structured information across business and healthcare environments, making information security and organizational risk management critical to reliable digital transactions. This study empirically assesses security-control preparedness and organizational risk-taking ability in EDI-related environments through a comparative analysis of General IT Professionals (GIT) and Healthcare EDI Professionals (HEP). A quantitative, cross-sectional survey was conducted with 100 respondents, equally divided between the two groups. Security Control Preparedness (SCP) and Threat Perception (TP) were validated using reliability and exploratory factor analyses, followed by group comparisons, correlation analysis, and multiple linear regression. HEP respondents reported significantly higher SCP than GIT respondents (M = 4.70 vs. 3.89, p < 0.001, d = 1.06) and higher Organizational Risk-Taking Ability (ORTA) (M = 6.14 vs. 5.28, p = 0.001, d = 0.66). In contrast, GIT respondents reported higher threat perception (M = 2.95 vs. 2.04, p < 0.001, d = 0.72). SCP was positively associated with ORTA (r = 0.479, p < 0.001), whereas TP showed a significant negative association (r = -0.610, p < 0.001). A parsimonious regression model incorporating SCP and TP explained 41.6% of the variance in ORTA (R^2 = 0.416, adjusted R^2 = 0.404, p < 0.001). Based on these empirical findings, an EDI Risk Management Framework (EDI-RMF) is proposed integrating governance, human and organizational security, technical safeguards, physical protection, and continuous monitoring. The findings provide empirical evidence for strengthening information-security risk management in EDI-enabled environments, particularly within developing-economy contexts.

Title: Towards Efficient Attack Tree Generation from Attack Graphs for Unobservable Target Analysis

  • Authors: Chijioke Eze, Aliyu Tanko Ali, Leonard Chidiebere Eze, Antonello Monti, Damas Gruska, Martin Leucker
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31302
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31302
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31302]] Towards Efficient Attack Tree Generation from Attack Graphs for Unobservable Target Analysis(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31302)
  • Keywords: security, attack
  • Abstract: Attack trees provide a structured approach to security analysis but traditionally require prior knowledge of the attacker's objective. In practice, this objective often remains hidden, even when some attacker actions are partially detected. This paper presents a methodology for generating attack trees without assuming a predefined target by modeling the threat environment as a constraint-weighted graph and applying temporal reachability analysis. The approach identifies probable attacker locations from partial observations and a time budget, carrying traversal cost as an attack-tree attribute. It then extracts target-oriented subgraphs that preserve feasible attack paths while eliminating irrelevant nodes. We demonstrate the framework across three scenarios: a single office building, a multi-building industrial complex, and a real IoT device-communication network recovered from the CICIoT2023 attack dataset. Compared with the baselines, our approach achieves a lower reduction factor while maintaining complete target preservation, substantially outperforming topology-only methods that ignore temporal constraints. We further perform a sensitivity analysis across 66 weight configurations, showing that target identification is stable when strategic value receives at least 20\% of the weight in the single building and at least 50\% in the multi-building complex, while additional experiments reveal sensitivity to perturbations of the underlying strategic-value prior.

Title: Scoring Recovery-Time Agent Security: Proposals, Effects, and Retry Instructions

  • Authors: Paul Deepakraj Retinraj
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31318
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31318
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31318]] Scoring Recovery-Time Agent Security: Proposals, Effects, and Retry Instructions(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31318)
  • Keywords: security, defense, attack
  • Abstract: A timed-out tool write may be absent, committed, or still in flight. Evaluating an agent only on whether its goal exists can hide harmful recovery actions: auditing 12 supplied traces changes apparent completion from 10/12 to 8/12. We introduce an executable evaluation that separately records tool proposals, runtime decisions, and committed effects. In 3,456 model episodes, the original task wording produces only 39/576 valid recoveries when the initial write is absent; a combined clarification produces 576/576. A further 1,584 episodes test stronger attacks and separate the effect definition from the recovery recipe with three rollouts per absent-state cell. Definition alone yields 276/288 valid recoveries; recipe alone yields 288/288. For Luna, malicious text is associated with 11.1 percentage points fewer valid recoveries under both original and definition-only wording, an availability signal limited by shared tasks and templates. The stronger attacks produce no attempted unauthorized or changed-key action and no unsafe effect. A trajectory audit finds six original-key retries after observing pending status: five denied and one deduplicated. Bounded checks distinguish atomic key enforcement from a delayed-commit race; stale negative reads also invalidate the evidence gate. A separate Claude Code program adds 720 successful episodes: 288 under the original attacks and 432 stronger-attack calibration episodes across Opus, Sonnet, and Haiku 5.5, with no observed proposal or effect deviations. The contribution is a measurement design and a diagnosis of availability, specification, and contract sensitivity, with no demonstrated general defense efficacy.

Title: Layer-Readout Interfaces in EEG Foundation Models: Predictive Accessibility and Cross-Dataset Transfer

  • Authors: Yanlin Fu, Wei Liu, Liming Zhao
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31320
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31320
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31320]] Layer-Readout Interfaces in EEG Foundation Models: Predictive Accessibility and Cross-Dataset Transfer(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31320)
  • Keywords: extraction
  • Abstract: Intermediate layers often outperform final layers when foundation models are used as frozen feature extractors, but the source of this advantage is unclear. We study this question in EEG foundation models by separating three factors: how representations are read out, whether intermediate layers provide predictive utility beyond the final-layer interface, and whether useful depth structure transfers across datasets. Across five models and three tasks, improving the final-layer readout consistently recovers substantial predictive performance, yet intermediate layers remain advantageous within frozen extraction. We also find that layers selected on one dataset remain useful on another across five dataset pairs and four task families, and that a compact set of source-derived task directions retains predictive value after transfer in sleep staging. Together, these results show that predictive utility varies across depth, and its usefulness depends on how representations are read out and transferred. More crucially, source-selected layers provide a reusable feature interface across datasets, reducing the need for target-side layer search while outperforming final-layer baselines on target datasets.

Title: Trustworthy Edge AI: Security, Privacy, and Resilience for Distributed Intelligence

  • Authors: Jaydip Sen, Subhasis Dasgupta
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.NI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31331
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31331
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31331]] Trustworthy Edge AI: Security, Privacy, and Resilience for Distributed Intelligence(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31331)
  • Keywords: secure, security, privacy, attack, robust, federate
  • Abstract: This chapter examines the principles and emerging research directions for building secure, privacy-preserving, and resilient Edge Artificial Intelligence (Edge AI) systems. As intelligence shifts from centralized clouds to distributed edge environments, challenges arise in ensuring data integrity, privacy, and reliable operation under adversarial and uncertain conditions. The chapter analyzes Edge AI architectures, emphasizing the expanded attack surface due to heterogeneous devices, resource constraints, and dynamic networks. It reviews core security mechanisms, including hardware-rooted trust, zero-trust models, secure communication, and attestation. Case studies from smart cities, autonomous vehicles, healthcare, and industrial IoT illustrate practical trade-offs. The chapter advocates a holistic, cross-layer design integrating security, privacy, and resilience for trustworthy edge intelligence.

Title: Per-Attack Blocking Hides Catalog Vulnerability: A Weakest-Link Analysis of Tool Manifests

  • Authors: Karthik Karunanithi
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31338
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31338
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31338]] Per-Attack Blocking Hides Catalog Vulnerability: A Weakest-Link Analysis of Tool Manifests(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31338)
  • Keywords: protect, attack
  • Abstract: Runtime controls for tool-using agents depend on manifests that state what each tool may read and where its output may go. Evaluations of these controls, including ChainCaps, report per-attack blocking: the share of attacks a set of manifests stops. We show that this is the wrong unit. A catalog is only as safe as its weakest source policy: if $M$ independently authored policies are each wrong with probability $p$, the chance that the catalog contains an accepted exfiltration path is $1-(1-p)^M$, while per-attack blocking stays at $1-p$. Three lines of evidence agree. In synthetic catalogs run through two enforcement engines, ChainCaps and OpenAPPA, per-attack blocking holds at 92% while catalog vulnerability rises from 22% at 10 tools to 99% at 200, and composition provides no protective effect. A fifteen-model authoring pilot shows that real errors cluster by author, so a 200-tool catalog behaves like about 5 independent decisions rather than 60 (95% interval 3.4 to 9.2). On 114 real MCP tools with expert-written rules, the models' own manifests block 89% of attacks yet leave 9 of 15 catalogs open, as the author-effect model predicts and independence does not. Evaluations should report catalog vulnerability, the sink coverage of their tests, and an effective number of independent decisions alongside per-attack blocking.

Title: Look Where Camouflage Breaks: Concealment-Guided Observation for Camouflaged Object Detection

  • Authors: Dingming Zhang, Fengyang Xiao, Jingjia Feng, Chunming He, Sina Farsiu
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31392
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31392

  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: A camouflaged object can blend into its surroundings at one scale and become distinguishable at another. Yet a region's apparent ambiguity gives limited guidance on which observation would resolve it. The challenge is to connect the form and scale of concealment to the evidence that a new view can reveal. We introduce CamoBreak, an active framework for camouflaged object detection guided by a Concealment Spectrum. From the image and current predictions, the spectrum measures regional resemblance and boundary ambiguity across comparison scales, distinguishing persistent concealment from separation emerging at finer or broader scales. These measurements guide crop selection from the already-captured image and recognition against global foreground and background references. An object-conditioned spatial preference then selects where local evidence should update the prediction and feature memory, retaining target corrections while suppressing background distractions. Accepted corrections reshape the next measurement, while predicted segmentation benefit determines whether to observe again. Experiments across encoder families and related segmentation tasks show consistent improvements. Targeted analyses support improved small-target recovery, multiple-object coverage, and selective evidence integration. Code will be released.

Title: CoRe: Jointly Learning Contextual Representations for Understanding and Generation

  • Authors: Hangke Sui, Chang Li, Yuyan Li, Mohammad Haris Baig, Daniel Ulbricht
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31397
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31397
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31397]] CoRe: Jointly Learning Contextual Representations for Understanding and Generation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31397)
  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: We introduce CoRe, in which understanding and generation jointly shape one contextual representation for images and one for text, trained from scratch. Each representation is read for understanding, conditions the other modality, and is the space in which that modality is generated by a continuous flow from Gaussian noise. With 383M parameters trained on CC12M and no pretrained language model, text encoder, or VAE at inference, CoRe matches a same-data contrastive-only baseline in global understanding and reaches COCO-30K FID 10.10, comparable to much larger generators built on pretrained components. In controlled comparisons at equal data and architecture, CoRe generates better in both directions (text to image and image to text) than a representation shaped by understanding alone and then frozen, and follows prompts better than generating in a space that understanding does not shape. In the other direction, with an attentive segmentation probe, on monocular depth, and when read by a frozen language model, CoRe surpasses the contrastive-only baseline: generation adds information that stronger readouts reach. Because generation runs inside the understanding representation, frozen real-image probes read and steer intermediate states and the model ranks its own samples without extra training. Because both modalities use continuous flows, both also distill to a few steps. Our controls indicate why one representation suffices: understanding organizes the representation semantically, while generation makes it retain more information.

Title: Leaks by Installments: Cross-Session Answer Disclosure in LLM Tutors and a Ledger-Based Defense

  • Authors: Venkata Muttineni, Karthik Narayan, Christopher David
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.CY
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31429
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31429
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31429]] Leaks by Installments: Cross-Session Answer Disclosure in LLM Tutors and a Ledger-Based Defense(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31429)
  • Keywords: defense, large language model
  • Abstract: Tutors built on large language models (LLMs) are told not to give away answers, but a learner does not have to ask for the answer all at once. Existing safeguards judge each reply within the current conversation, so a learner can open a new session, reword the problem, or request a worked solution to the same problem with different numbers. We call this cross-session disclosure and propose a defense: a persistent, per-learner disclosure ledger. For every problem a learner brings, the system builds a typed solution graph. Before a reply is released, a gate matches the reply's own content, not the learner's wording, against the ledger by result values and by type-labeled solution structure, then computes which steps the learner could now derive. The reply is withheld if that set reaches the final answer or exceeds a budget. In a simulation of 3,000 problems, the ledger reduced leakage through renumbered practice variants from 100% to 10.6% while honest learners completed 94-99.5% of problems. With two real LLM tutors (Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct and Phi-3.5-mini-instruct) on 60 problems, leakage through renumbered variants fell from 0.58 to 0.08 and from 1.00 to 0.18, with 98% honest completion. The ledger did not reduce split requests beyond a per-session gate in the live runs. All acceptance criteria were fixed before each run.

  • Keywords: robust, transformer
  • Abstract: In-context learning (ICL) enables transformers to adapt to new tasks from demonstrations without updating their parameters. However, theoretical guarantees for transformers remain limited in general, and elusive in particular when target responses depend nonlinearly on the evolution of observed paths. We establish an ICL framework for path space, using path signature features. Building on the universal linear approximation property of signatures, we construct explicitly a causal softmax transformer with two attention heads per layer that emulates in-context Lasso.   • Abstract: This paper is concerned with prompt-image alignment at inference time through exploration of generative trajectories and dynamical prompt rejuvenation. Title: Your Codec, Your Generator: Idempotent Generative Compression without Retraining

  • Authors: Raz Haim, Assaf Shocher
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31520
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31520
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31520]] Your Codec, Your Generator: Idempotent Generative Compression without Retraining(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31520)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: We introduce an image compression framework with three properties: (i) adaptation of pretrained codecs without retraining or fine-tuning, (ii) cycle-consistent recompression, even of already-encoded images, and (iii) decoding with independently chosen generative priors. We wrap the frozen encoder with non-learned operations so that every continuous latent has a preimage, yielding a surjective pseudo-invertible neural network. A nonlinear right inverse built from the pretrained decoder satisfies $E'(D'(E'(x)))=E'(x)$. To extend consistency to quantized bitstreams, the receiver searches for a continuous latent that reproduces both the transmitted main symbols and entropy side information. A successful search and an exact correction preserve both streams under deterministic coding; the final reconstruction is checked through the actual coding path. The encoder's pseudo-inverse enables a nonlinear counterpart of back-projection. In the linear case, back-projection has the closed-form solution $x'=x+A^\dagger(y-Ax)$, giving the closest reconstruction to $x$ satisfying $Ax'=y$. Our nonlinear formulation enforces the transmitted code on proposals from an independently chosen generative model. Adjusting the starting noise controls the prior's influence without changing that code. Experiments show small first-pass quality changes, higher retained quality than trained consistency codecs over 50 recompressions, also under 8-bit storage, and decoder-side control of the perception-distortion trade-off. Decoupling the codec from its prior lets the receiver adopt future generators without retraining or re-encoding.

Title: Diagnosing Epistemic Uncertainty in Neural Operators: Effects of Initialization, Discretization, and Function Sampling

  • Authors: Tian Yu Yen, Yucheng Fu, Pratanu Roy, Guang Lin, Michael Penwarden
  • Subjects: cs.LG, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31526
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31526
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31526]] Diagnosing Epistemic Uncertainty in Neural Operators: Effects of Initialization, Discretization, and Function Sampling(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31526)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Neural operators provide efficient surrogates for mappings between function spaces, but their reliability cannot be characterized by the performance of a single trained model. Existing neural operator uncertainty quantification approaches often characterize predictive uncertainty without separately identifying the contributions of individual sources of epistemic uncertainty. Finite sampling of the training function space, output discretization, and model initialization can produce substantial variation among learned operators even when the underlying physical problem is deterministic. We systematically investigate these sources of epistemic uncertainty across a diverse set of operator learning architectures. Across our experiments, we train approximately 46,000 neural operator models and construct ensembles over training datasets, model initializations, and output discretizations to characterize distributions of relative $L_2$ error rather than individual trained realizations. We find substantial architecture-dependent differences in epistemic uncertainty, with model initialization often accounting for the largest proportion of variance in relative $L_2$ error. The ranked performance of neural operator architectures can invert as the number and spatial distribution of output samples change, with the best architectures under sparse or randomly sampled observations differing from those at higher-resolution or structured discretizations. Furthermore, ensembles that reliably characterize aggregate operator error can provide unreliable estimates of error quantiles for individual test functions. These results highlight the importance of distinguishing the sources of epistemic uncertainty when evaluating neural operator reliability and establishing robust benchmarking practices.

Title: Aftershock: Yesterday's Linux Kernel Bugs, Tomorrow's Android Exploits

We test this threat end to end by building an automated pipeline that screens upstream Linux patches, reproduces memory-safety vulnerabilities on Android targets, and drives an LLM agent to construct local privilege escalation exploits from an untrusted application. For these 17 CVEs, the corresponding upstream patches reach Pixel devices with a median delay of 181 days. Title: Denoising Diffusion with Progressive Posterior Sampling

  • Authors: Zeyu Luo, Shuai Huang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31558
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31558
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31558]] Denoising Diffusion with Progressive Posterior Sampling(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31558)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Diffusion models provide effective generative priors for solving noisy linear inverse problems. However, existing posterior sampling methods may require singular value decomposition (SVD) of the measurement operator or prior knowledge of the measurement noise variance, while guidance based on local gradients provides only a limited view of posterior geometry. We propose progressive posterior sampling (PPS) with manifold projection, which automatically estimates the measurement noise variance and avoids SVD. During reverse diffusion, PPS constructs a Gaussian approximation to the posterior distribution in closed form and guides a sample drawn from the local prior toward the approximate posterior mean. We then project the guided sample onto a spherical level set that approximates the local noisy image manifold, promoting consistency with the local geometry of the diffusion prior. Experiments on FFHQ and ImageNet across four restoration tasks and three measurement noise levels demonstrate competitive reconstruction accuracy and perceptual quality by PPS.

Title: Spec$^2$R: Recovering Emergent Properties in Supervised Vision Transformers

  • Authors: Adam Wróbel, Jacek Tabor, Bartosz Zieliński, Marcin Przewięźlikowski
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31589
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31589
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31589]] Spec$^2$R: Recovering Emergent Properties in Supervised Vision Transformers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31589)
  • Keywords: robust, transformer
  • Abstract: Vision Transformers (ViTs) have achieved strong performance across computer vision tasks, but the quality of their learned representations differs drastically depending on the training paradigm. While self-supervised ViTs often demonstrate emergent properties, such as object-aligned patch representations and attention maps, supervised ViTs typically lack these properties, even when trained with strong data augmentations. Understanding the origin of this difference is important for explaining and controlling the object-level structure. To investigate it, we perform a spectral analysis of supervised and self-supervised models, and identify a key distinction: MLP layers in supervised ViTs introduce high-frequency variations into patch representations, disrupting their spatial organization. Spec$^2$R recovers object-aligned patch representations and attention maps, while improving adversarial robustness and preserving competitive classification accuracy.

Title: LUCID: Statistically Grounded Self-Correction for LLM Reasoning Evaluation

  • Authors: Satiyabooshan Murugaboopathy, Adel Daoud, Connor T. Jerzak
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.LG, stat.ME
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31595
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31595
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31595]] LUCID: Statistically Grounded Self-Correction for LLM Reasoning Evaluation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31595)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Cheap language-model raters grade chain-of-thought reasoning at scale, yet systematic step-rating errors can skew reasoning audits and solution rankings. We introduce LUCID (Loop for Unbiased Calibration via Inference-time Diagnosis), which pairs prompt self-correction with Design-based Supervised Learning (DSL). Without retraining or weight access, LUCID calibrates binary step ratings by aligning predicted and expert soundness rates. From a small expert-labeled set, LUCID summarizes rater bias in plain text and prompts the rater to re-grade unseen steps. For example, on PRM800K, GPT-5-mini grades too strictly, under-rating computation, verification, and late-trace steps most acutely. In one pass, LUCID shrinks GPT-5-mini's calibration gap from 7.8 to 0.9 percentage points in absolute predicted-expert rate difference. LUCID also halves false alarms (sound steps rated unsound) and lifts error-detection F1 on unsound steps by 32%. LUCID retains these gains with 1,000 diagnostic steps or overall-gap feedback alone. The loop also transfers across models and domains: it applies GPT-5-mini's bias profile to lift GLM-5.2's PRM800K accuracy, and it narrows calibration gaps for overly strict but capable raters across math, science, web, and logic benchmarks. Because prompt feedback shifts thresholds without improving class-balanced sound/unsound discrimination, LUCID filters re-grading with pre-/post-checks that skip already calibrated raters and roll back ineffective passes. Finally, we reuse that expert set with raw corpus ratings in DSL for calibration and regression debiasing, and apply DSL to pair-labeled steps for sound-step transitions. DSL's out-of-fold soundness probabilities lift best-of-K selection to match expert labels, while its doubly-robust estimators beat expert-only regression fits at small budgets and cut sound-step transition error by nearly two-thirds.

Title: Noise-Level Adjacency in Diffusion Training

  • Authors: T. Y. Tsui, Jiatao Gu, Lingjie Liu
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31605
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31605
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31605]] Noise-Level Adjacency in Diffusion Training(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31605)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Diffusion models train one network across all noise levels, although their training objective is pointwise in the noise level. If training on each pair of adjacent levels were an independent problem, separate networks with the same total parameter count would be expected to match one shared network. But in practice, on images, sharing weights across levels gives better results, which hints at an implicit dependence on their order. An order fixes both which levels are adjacent and the direction in which they run. In this paper, we show that, of the two, the benefit of sharing rests on adjacency in the noise-level embedding. The fitting error of a shared network is bounded below by the distances between neighbouring posterior means, minus the change the network can make between neighbouring embeddings. We first distinguish adjacency from direction: on a Gaussian mixture and CIFAR-10, reversing the embeddings keeps every neighbour and leaves the fitting error unchanged, whereas shuffling them raises it. Second, we rule out sharing between distant levels as the source of the benefit: separate networks on contiguous ranges match the shared network at its width, and with the parameters fixed, breaking adjacency at the same places costs less than 40\% of a full shuffle. Dividing the levels at equal shares of these distances lowers the error of all but the smallest networks. We therefore show that the order of the noise levels enters a diffusion model through adjacency, with its direction being a design choice. Our code is available at this https URL.

Title: Evaluating LLM Security Review Agents with Operationally Sourced Ground Truth

  • Authors: Mudita Khurana, Karim Liman-Tinguiri
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31606
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31606
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31606]] Evaluating LLM Security Review Agents with Operationally Sourced Ground Truth(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31606)
  • Keywords: security
  • Abstract: LLM-based security review agents can reason about vulnerabilities that deterministic tools miss, but deploying them responsibly requires knowing whether they actually work. Existing benchmarks construct ground truth synthetically or from public CVE databases and are blind to the vulnerability patterns specific to a given production codebase. We present an evaluation framework grounded in an organisation's own vulnerabilities, with three contributions. First, a dataset construction methodology that derives ground truth from operational security data (bug bounty reports, penetration test findings and internally reported vulnerabilities), exploiting the property that a confirmed vulnerability must exist immediately before its fix is merged and be absent immediately after. Second, two complementary evaluation strategies: one reviewing the pull request that introduced a vulnerability and one searching a repository where it already exists. Both run through a two-agent pipeline pairing a Security Engineer agent, which reviews code without knowledge of the vulnerability, with a Judge agent that decides whether its findings would have mitigated the known issue. Third, a trace-level behavioural characterisation of how frontier models reason about these vulnerabilities. Across GPT-5.5, Sonnet 4.6 and Opus 4.7, GPT-5.5 shows higher discovery rates on the pull-request strategy (57%, 95% CI [42%, 72%]) after a round of OPRO-style improvements, and Sonnet 4.6 on the repository strategy (69%, 95% CI [56%, 81%]). Trace analysis shows the models have complementary strengths that explain these differences, and that outcome-only scoring can mischaracterise their production behaviour. The methodology relies only on artefacts that vulnerability management workflows commonly produce, though all results come from a single organisation's codebase and we have not tested how well they generalise.

  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31622

  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31622]] Internal inconsistencies and unresolved experimental evidence in a quantum neurosecurity framework(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31622)
  • Keywords: secure, security, privacy
  • Abstract: This article presents a technical and theoretical assessment of the Quantum-Entangled Neuro-Symbolic Swarm Federation (QENSSF), a framework intended for privacy-preserving, quantum-secure, AI-powered, multimodal healthcare data analysis, introduced by Ben Othman and Ali (2025). They claim QENSSF is a first-in-class framework with provable privacy and security guarantees, support these with theorems and proofs, report an experimental evaluation on IBM's quantum Eagle processor, report a real-world quantum key distribution (QKD) test, and claim use of real-world multimodal datasets (ADNI, UK Biobank, and MIMIC-IV). QENSSF identifies a real need: ensuring privacy and security of healthcare data while patients benefit from AI-powered analysis. Privacy concerns also extend to wearable EEG data, for which reidentification risks can persist despite deidentification. However, QENSSF's foundational inconsistencies need resolution before it could be considered in the neurosecurity domain.

Title: What You See Is What You Certify: Concept-Based Certificates for Unseen Compositions

  • Authors: Phuc Linh Ngo, Thang Nguyen Duc, Truong Thanh Hung Nguyen, Hung Cao
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31627
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31627
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31627]] What You See Is What You Certify: Concept-Based Certificates for Unseen Compositions(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31627)
  • Keywords: fair
  • Abstract: Concept-based models justify a decision by concepts they claim to see, decoded through an explicit ontology. A decision decoded from such concepts can satisfy the ontology and still be wrong, so we ask when it can serve as a certificate. On unseen combinations of familiar parts, a joint concept bottleneck model and a concept embedding model, trained as is standard, certify wrong classes 93-94% of the time despite a correct ontology (60% for a joint model trained only on visible parts and gated by a localizer). On CUB, isolated region readers are more accurate than an attribute-prototype model (63.1% vs. 55.3%) and certify unseen species at about 6 points less risk than it and about 10 points less than features pooled in the same keypoint boxes. On real traffic signs, isolated readers lower certified risk from 99.8% to 67%, which is still far too high; most remaining errors are misread pictograms. On seen classes the ontology brings no gain over a fairly trained black box, and isolation costs accuracy.

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Time series transformers have achieved strong forecasting performance, yet it remains unclear which of their components, attention or the feed-forward network (FFN), constructs temporal features, and how tokenization such as patching changes this. It targets models whose probed representations are read by a linear forecasting head. Applied to 5 supervised models trained on 8 long-term forecasting benchmarks, it finds that the constructing component is not fixed but allocated by the interaction of tokenization and architecture. In probing, attention dominates under variate tokenization (iTransformer); in CATS, cross-attention is the only input path. In a controlled manipulation of PatchTST, single-scalar tokens (P=1) nearly eliminate the FFN's construction, while one more point per token (P=2) restores it. Our forecasting-side interventions (activation patching, knockout, and training without attention) establish component-level reliance and sufficiency. These results offer a representational reading of prior findings on the gains from patching, the competitiveness of self-attention-free models (TSMixer, PatchMLP), and the distinct role of cross-attention in CATS.

  • Authors: Li Qiwei, Salma Abdel Magid, Olga Russakovsky, Eric Gilbert, Sarita Schoenebeck

  • Abstract: Adversarial perturbation defenses protect images from unauthorized edits by adding imperceptible noise. We also show that standard evaluation metrics such as peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR) are essentially uncorrelated with nudification protection. BODYBENCH makes this misalignment measurable and provides a foundation for defenses grounded in documented harm.

  • Subjects: cs.CV, eess.IV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31777

  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: The Navier-Stokes (NS) inpainting model of Bertalmío, Bertozzi and Sapiro is the only transport-type paradigm among PDE-based methods, yet it has never received a systematic higher-order extension. We show that the obvious fourth-order correction is ill-posed, and we construct the corrected, well-posed model. In the vorticity-streamfunction formulation we add a gated fourth-order term whose sign is decisive: the backward form is a Perona-Malik-type anti-diffusion, Hadamard ill-posed and consistently harmful in experiments, whereas the forward form is uniformly parabolic, energy-stable and consistently beneficial. An order-dissipation principle, stating that odd orders are dispersive and even orders dissipative, singles out the fourth order as the lowest admissible one. Large missing blocks pose a separate problem, because the correction gate stays silent there; we answer it with stripe decomposition, a Telea base fill followed by interleaved slit-wise NS passes, which converts block damage into the structural-slit regime where the model excels. The device is base-agnostic, and its gains add to those of the forward correction. On three datasets, namely BSDS500, DIV2K and a classical Chinese painting benchmark, and on three damage types, namely cracks, blocks and text occlusion, the training-free model beats every classical baseline on every sample of structure-dominated damage, gains +1.02 dB PSNR over Telea on the full 42-image painting test set with a 42/42 per-sample win rate, matches the strongest recent PDE solvers within 0.4 dB while attaining the best perceptual quality of the entire PDE family, and runs at 4-16 s per image on a consumer GPU.

  • Abstract: Hyperdimensional computing (HDC) models deployed on edge hardware are valuable intellectual property that is easy to steal, since a trained classifier consolidates its learned knowledge into a compact set of class prototype hypervectors; yet protecting their ownership through watermarking remains largely unexplored. We further identify a dimension-permutation attack specific to HDC, which reorders hypervector coordinates to evade verification without altering predictions, and counter it through encoder realignment. Existing methods struggle to reconcile these dimensions: prompting succumbs to context drift over long sequences, while fine-tuning often suppresses idiosyncratic "quirks" in favor of generic distributional patterns. To bridge this gap, we present QuirkyMind, a framework that disentangles identity definition from its expression. First, Traits Anchoring constructs a dual-stream latent state, fusing a sentence-level summary for semantic stability with a token-level sequence for generative control. This state is stabilized via In-Context Narrative Refinement using an alternating objective: a discriminative InfoNCE loss anchors the persona in representation space to prevent drift, while a generative cross-entropy loss ensures faithful verbalization. Finally, Persona Steered Generalization transfers the refined state to downstream tasks via parameter-efficient adapters. Empirical evaluations on Persona-Steered QA and Narrative Inference demonstrate that QuirkyMind mitigates drift, consolidating persona knowledge without erasing authentic incongruities.

Title: The Oracle Is Not Enough: Secure Settlement for Prediction Markets

  • Authors: Jiayu Liu, Sen Yang, Xueyuan Zhao, Kartik Nayak, Fan Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.GT

  • Keywords: secure, security
  • Abstract: Prediction markets are becoming an important mechanism for aggregating information and forecasting uncertain real-world events. In this paper, we ask how to settle heterogeneous markets correctly and efficiently at scale. Title: Subliminal Learning Crosses Modalities

  • Authors: Saraswathy Amjith, Kimia Hamidieh, Antonio Torralba, Yoon Kim
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31870
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31870
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31870]] Subliminal Learning Crosses Modalities(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31870)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Subliminal learning refers to a surprising phenomenon in language models wherein a prompt given to one model (e.g., preference for cats) can be subliminally distilled to another model via training on data that is ostensibly meaningless (e.g., number sequences). This paper shows that text-based subliminal learning transfers cross-modally, in particular, a topic/preference (e.g., animals) transferred through number sequences across vision-language models also changes how images are generated and represented. First, we show that a diffusion model using the student as its text encoder generates the student's animal more often than with the original model as its text encoder, but not its color or style. We then investigate the mechanism behind this phenomenon and which concepts transfer across modalities. Adding this component uniformly can change the generated animal, which depends on the whole prompt, but not concepts such as color or style, which are encoded in a single token. Finally, we introduce weight lens as an approach to identify a subliminal trait from the weights of a single trained model, and show that it identifies the trait of nearly every student, including traits that do not surface in generated images.

Title: Beyond the Last Loop: Reinforcement Learning with a Teacher Extrapolated from the Model Itself

  • Authors: Linghua Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31939
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31939
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31939]] Beyond the Last Loop: Reinforcement Learning with a Teacher Extrapolated from the Model Itself(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31939)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Looped language models apply one shared transformer stack several times per token, and every loop can be read out through the shared language-model head. Post-training them with verifiable rewards is hard: the outcome reward carries one bit per response, spreading it over loops trains intermediate loops on tokens sampled from the last loop, and distillation needs either a teacher that ignores the reward or an external, larger model. We observe that a looped model already contains a teacher stronger than its last loop: the next-token distribution extrapolated from the first loop through the last. ELGO (Extrapolated-Loop Group-relative Optimization) adds to GRPO a dense per-token distillation term towards this distribution. The teacher is computed from an anchor copy of the model, which stays frozen and is replaced by the current model once the student's KL to the teacher falls below a threshold, so the teacher improves with the student without chasing its own extrapolation. The outcome reward splits probability between correct and incorrect responses, and the teacher decides which tokens are preferred inside a response. Between refreshes the objective is KL-regularised RL anchored at the teacher, whose optimum tilts the teacher by the reward. On Ouro-1.4B-Thinking, ELGO raises the average avg@8 over five math benchmarks from 62.32 with GRPO to 64.20 and is the best compared method on every benchmark, while spreading the reward over loops falls below the base model.

Title: Sounding the Black Box: Online Training of Embedded Neural Networks in Forward-Only Physical Simulators

  • Authors: Ruiqi Shu, Tianrun Gao, Haomin Wen, Xiaomeng Huang
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31964
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.31964
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31964]] Sounding the Black Box: Online Training of Embedded Neural Networks in Forward-Only Physical Simulators(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31964)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Hybrid physics-neural simulators embed neural networks in partial differential equation (PDE) solvers to model unresolved subgrid dynamics while preserving resolved physics and conservation laws. Because closure outputs feed back across time steps, single-step offline regression does not directly minimize multi-step rollout error, motivating online trajectory optimization. Solver backpropagation through time (BPTT) is the standard online solution when solver derivatives are available, yet many operational Fortran and C/C++ simulators expose only non-differentiable, forward-only interfaces. We introduce FORGE (Forward-Only Response and Gauss-Newton Estimation), an online framework that trains embedded networks from forward runs alone by decoupling exact network derivatives from black-box simulator dynamics. Stage I warms up the rollout via a low-dimensional Gauss-Newton step along output-orthonormal weight directions, canceling multi-layer feature drift via a final-layer correction. Stage II perturbs spatial output fields to fit a gain-bounded causal linear response operator across rollout steps, solves for regularized target outputs, and updates all weights via network backpropagation. Across five 2D and 3D benchmarks spanning phase separation, reaction-diffusion, fluid and MHD turbulence, and ocean circulation, FORGE outperforms gradient-free, reinforcement-learning, and emulator-based baselines, cutting coarse-solver test mean squared error (MSE) by 50%-96% in 2D and 70% in 3D, outperforming the strongest forward-only baseline by 16%-69%, and recovering 88%-98% of Solver BPTT's gains. Code is available at this https URL.

  • Abstract: Looped transformers reuse one block of shared weights along the depth of the network. Recent looped diffusion transformers are trained from scratch, which repeats the full pretraining cost although strong pretrained non-looped diffusion transformers exist. We propose Distill to Loop (Dooop), which distills a pretrained non-looped diffusion transformer into a looped student whose blocks are initialized from the teacher's. We cast distillation as a fixed-point iteration toward the teacher, in which the output after every loop is trained toward the teacher, and each loop is one step of an ordinary differential equation in depth. Meanwhile, because a looped model produces an output after every loop, we also construct loop guidance between the outputs of different loop counts, which improves the generated samples at little extra compute. On SD3-medium, a looped student with about 37% of the teacher's parameters matches the teacher on average over GenEval, ImageReward, PickScore and HPSv2.1 with two to four loops, and with two loops it uses about 58% of the teacher's compute. Therefore, a physics-inspired wave evolution attention framework is proposed, namely WaveSR. The WaveSR framework involves two key modules, i.e., wave evolution attention (WEA) and Frequency-Adaptive Feed-Forward Network (FA-FFN). Title: Reconstructing Natural Images from Brain Activity during Natural Vision

  • Authors: Tejasvi Kothapalli, Ryan Ressmeyer, Declan Rowley, Gregory D. Horwitz, Jude Mitchell, Alexander C. Huk, Bruno Olshausen, Jacob Yates
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31989

  • Copy Paste: [[2611.31989]] Reconstructing Natural Images from Brain Activity during Natural Vision(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31989)

  • Abstract: Reconstructing images from brain activity has two broad goals. The first is to build brain-computer interfaces. The second is to understand how the brain sees. Image reconstruction lets us test which natural image prior best matches the brain's natural image prior. Three factors determine reconstruction quality: the amount of neural evidence combined over viewing time, the accuracy of eye tracking, and the choice of image prior. Our method sets a new state-of-the-art performance in reconstructing natural images from brain activity, across brain recording modalities: fMRI, EEG, MEG and electrophysiology.

Large language models (LLMs) have advanced automated patch generation, yet passing functionality tests does not ensure that a patch enforces the security requirement the vulnerability violated. Existing vulnerability repair techniques rely on curated patterns or templates with limited coverage, leaving unclear which repair strategies carry over across vulnerabilities and how they can steer LLMs. We present a large-scale empirical study that relates recurring fix patterns to the security impacts of vulnerabilities and examines how this knowledge can guide LLM-based repair. Building on a manual study of security patches, we cluster 3,816 security patches from 348 open-source repositories and analyze the edits and their context to derive concrete fix patterns, ranging from local safeguards to coordinated changes across multiple locations. We define 12 security impact classes by the security requirements that vulnerabilities violate and map them to repair categories and fix patterns, revealing characteristic repairs, alternative strategies within a class, and patterns shared across classes. We then use these patterns and impact-pattern associations to select repair examples for four LLMs on repair tasks of varying complexity. Guided example selection raises the four-model mean pass@3 from 37.9% under zero-shot prompting to up to 49.2%, whereas randomly selected examples reach only 36.9%. Our findings connect vulnerability characterization with patch generation and support organizing repair knowledge around security requirements.

Title: Distractors are the Real Enemy: Visual Distraction Adversarial Perturbations on SAM2-Based Video Tracking and Segmentation

  • Authors: Adonay Demewez Gebremedhin, Mohamad Alansari, Wessam Shehieb, Muzammal Naseer

  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32011]] Distractors are the Real Enemy: Visual Distraction Adversarial Perturbations on SAM2-Based Video Tracking and Segmentation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32011)
  • Keywords: attack, robust, segmentation
  • Abstract: Promptable video trackers such as SAM2 achieve accurate and robust performance through strong temporal consistency, making them important foundations for downstream video understanding and tracking applications. As their use continues to expand, understanding their robustness to adversarial perturbations becomes increasingly important. Existing attacks on SAM2 largely focus on local mask degradation and short-term temporal feature disruption, leaving its longer-term dynamics and competing object interferences (Distractors) insufficiently explored. We observe that SAM2 itself exposes useful distractor cues when tracking becomes uncertain. Based on these observations, we propose DistractAttack, a white-box attack that uses background distractors to disrupt target tracking persistently. Its core procedure, Counterfactual Distractor Search (CDS), propagates clean and attacked trajectories in parallel to discover plausible competing regions and identify those that become increasingly effective under perturbation. The discovered evidence is accumulated over time to weaken target support and promote recurrent alternatives or disappearance predictions. Extensive experiments under video-level and one-shot perturbations across Visual Object Tracking and Video Object Segmentation demonstrate strong attack effectiveness, raising DRE to $4.4\times$ and NRE to $7.3\times$ their nearest baselines under video perturbation, while transferring effectively across model scales and tracker variants in black-box settings. Title: To Critic Or Not To Critic

  • Authors: Jason Wei, George Pu, Jingxuan Fan, Yuan Emily Xue, Yuan Christy Li
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32013
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32013
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32013]] To Critic Or Not To Critic(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32013)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: As large language models tackle longer and more complex tasks, understanding when a critic improves the efficiency of reinforcement learning (RL) becomes increasingly important. Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) trains a critic to reduce variance and provide fine-grained credit assignment, while Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) drops the critic and avoids the bias of bootstrapping from the critic's value estimates. Although recent critic-based methods have reported strong performance relative to GRPO, it remains unclear when each approach is preferable. We argue that there is no universal answer; instead, the choice depends on several interacting factors, including batch size, critic reliance, and task complexity. We study the bias-variance tradeoff in terms of gradient direction, measuring how closely each method's gradient estimates align with the true policy gradient as batch size grows. Under a shared actor objective, we compare GRPO with PPO in control tasks, short-horizon math, and long-horizon terminal agents. Across more than 1,200 training runs, including 96 with LLMs, we find that PPO generally leads at small batch sizes, while GRPO often overtakes heavily bootstrapped PPO and reaches parity with lightly bootstrapped PPO at larger batches. At longer horizons and in more complex domains, GRPO needs larger batches to catch up to PPO's performance. Accounting for training compute, critics pay off at small budgets, but their returns diminish at larger budgets.

Title: APQKE-DR: Entropy-Failure Analysis of a Post-Quantum-Initialized Ratcheted Channel

  • Authors: Muhammad Asfand Hafeez, Zain Ul Abideen, Amna Zafar, Kazi Hassan Shakib, Arslan Munir
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32024
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32024
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32024]] APQKE-DR: Entropy-Failure Analysis of a Post-Quantum-Initialized Ratcheted Channel(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32024)
  • Keywords: secure, security, attack
  • Abstract: Long-lived secure channels for embedded and cyber-physical systems (CPSs) must resist quantum attacks and failures in endpoint randomness. While several post-quantum key exchange protocols exist, their security assumes reliable randomness at the endpoint, but this assumption is not always true in embedded devices. In this paper, we introduce the Entropy Attack Suite (EAS) to evaluate secure channels facing endpoint randomness failures, such as low-entropy boot and Random Number Generation (RNG) path subversion. As a case study, we design and implement APQKE-DR, a post-quantum secure channel. Its handshake uses ML-KEM [1] and ML-DSA [2], producing a root key for an X25519 Double Ratchet. We assess the performance of APQKE-DR and security for smart grid communication, and integrate it beneath ANSI C12.22 and DNP3 without altering payload formats. On a Raspberry Pi Compute Module 5, the APQKE handshake exchanges 9,610 bytes with median latencies of 1.99 ms (node) and 0.82 ms (server). The ratcheted channel adds 31 bytes per message. With fresh randomness and ideal cryptographic primitives, ProVerif verifies the modeled secrecy and authentication properties. If randomness fails, EAS can recover the RNG stream and root key from public transcripts. Under RNG-path subversion, a weak generator exposes the root key across sessions. If subversion also affects X25519 keys, attackers may access ratchet keys and plaintexts. TPM seeding prevents early-boot attacks but not later subversion, while measured boot can block modified firmware. Secure deployment thus requires integrity along the entire entropy path, not just strong cryptographic primitives.
