Title: DeCo-Attention: Decompose to Compensate for Low-Precision Attention

  • Authors: Yuhang He, Harrison Zhang, Fanrong Li, Chang Liu, RuQing G. Xu, Perkz Zheng, Zhenhua Wang, Rajeev Rao, Mengdi Wang, Sharan Chetlur, Jun Yang
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32035
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32035
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32035]] DeCo-Attention: Decompose to Compensate for Low-Precision Attention(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32035)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Self-attention over tens of thousands of tokens dominates the generation time of video diffusion transformers, because its cost grows quadratically with sequence length. The FP4 matrix products of modern GPUs can speed up attention, but the 4-bit rounding of the queries Q and the keys K introduces large errors. We show that the exponential in softmax amplifies the error of their product QK, which therefore dominates the attention error. We propose DeCo-Attention, a co-design of algorithm and system for low-precision attention. DeCo-Attention is training-free and compensates the rounding error of Q and K along a few principal directions found by a per-head eigendecomposition. The lightweight compensation terms are appended to Q and K as extra channels, so the FP4 matrix product corrects its own rounding error while it computes the attention scores. Once the products run in FP4, the softmax bounds the kernel, and we optimize the softmax so that the gain of FP4 computation translates into realized speedup. With QK in FP4, we implement DeCo-Attention with the rest of attention in FP8 on GB300 and VR200 and in FP4 on RTX PRO 6000. On Wan2.2, these kernels run 2.13x, 2.49x, and 2.82x faster than BF16 attention, and the whole generation runs 1.55x, 1.68x, and 1.80x faster. On four video generation models, the FP8 configuration achieves LPIPS comparable to prior 8-bit QK methods, and the fully FP4 configuration lower LPIPS than prior fully 4-bit kernels.

Title: Online Environment Repair for Coding Agents

  • Authors: Sheng Lu, Rui Mao, Zewei Chen, Chihua Ying, Beiduo Chen, Dingxin Hu, Tinghao Yu
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CR, cs.SE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32037

  • Keywords: defense
  • Abstract: Programmatic verifiers provide scalable reward signals for training coding agents with reinforcement learning, yet subtle harness defects and incomplete unit tests allow policies to earn full credit without solving the intended task. Existing training-time defenses intervene either by penalizing suspicious outputs or by filtering flagged tasks and rollouts. However, output penalties leave the underlying verifier exploitable, driving policies to disguise shortcuts under reasoning monitors or zeroing out valid solutions under detector false positives, whereas task and rollout filtering discards or mutes the hardest problems where shortcuts emerge earliest. Pre-training verifier hardening repairs the environment directly, but misses vulnerability classes that surface only as the policy improves during reinforcement learning. In this paper, we propose online environment repair, which leaves triggering rollout rewards unchanged to remove hiding incentives and asynchronously patches exploited environments during training. Across 20 controlled GRPO experiments on heterogeneous software-engineering repositories, online environment repair reduces cumulative reward hacking from 36.9% to 0.06% and improves held-out Pass@8 from 21.7% to 46.7%, consistently outperforming reward-penalty, rollout-filtering, and static-hardening baselines while eliminating zero-shot exploitation on unseen loophole classes.

We further demonstrate, for the first time for continued fraction networks, that their effectiveness also carries over to image generative modeling, that utilize diffusion objectives. On a state-of-the-art Diffusion Transformer, we show that our approach again achieves performance comparable to the original FFN-based models with ~30% parameter savings and significantly outperforms the previous incremental training approach.

However, naively sharing parameters can disrupt the distinct transformations learned at different depths. To address this challenge, we identify suitable sharing groups based on inter-layer representation dissimilarity and keep the final backbone block independent for multi-scale feature construction. We then initialize each shared backbone block with the pretrained weights of its group's terminal layer to better preserve the learned representations. Subsequently, we distill visual representations into the looped backbone and integrate it with a looped decoder for task-specific training. Experiments demonstrate that LoopDVR achieves the best accuracy--parameter trade-offs across dense recognition tasks. Compared with the reference models, it reduces parameters by approximately 28% while maintaining competitive accuracy. Notably, LoopDet-S achieves 50.9 box AP with only 7.0M parameters, highlighting its effectiveness in compact models.

  • Abstract: The widespread deployment of latent generative models (LGMs) makes generated image source attribution essential for copyright auditing and data governance, yet practical attribution should remain reliable under common post-processing and platform-induced processing. These summaries discard channel-frequency structure, allowing image content and post-processing variation to obscure source evidence and making attribution after spreading difficult. We instead uncover a more stable source-discriminative cue under post-processing: the frequency-resolved evolution of residual energy across repeated applications of the same reconstruction operator. Motivated by this insight, we characterize a phenomenon we term Spectral Contraction and use it to build SpCon, a source-attribution framework that accesses only the target model's VAE. SpCon obtains adjacent residuals through two deterministic reconstructions, builds a spectral-contraction representation from their frequency-resolved relative energy change, and aggregates Student-t response densities for each source-condition pair over a finite set of fitted reference components. The same representation supports three settings according to non-belonging reference availability: none (Target-Only, TO; belonging references only), external out-of-domain sources (External-Reference, ER), or known in-domain competitors (Known-Reference, KR). Across seven LGMs, TO, ER, and KR attain average AUROC/AP of 0.8786/0.6344, 0.9404/0.7886, and 0.9652/0.8626 over the 35 conditions, all outperforming the strongest baseline (0.8154/0.5277), and achieve 0.8581/0.5507, 0.9369/0.7549, and 0.9734/0.8773 on the three social platforms.

  • Abstract: Text-to-scene methods turn a short instruction into a furnished 3D room, but the room that a particular person wants rarely comes across fully in words: many preferences surface only when the person sees a concrete room and can point to what they like and dislike in it. The system must therefore bring the whole room toward the user's preferences from feedback that comments on only part of it at a time. We present PreferenceScene, in which a large language model keeps an explicit estimate of the user's preferences for every design attribute of the room and, after each turn, infers the taste behind the feedback and revises the whole estimate, including the parts that the feedback does not mention. A scene instantiation module builds every estimated value as its own part of the room, so that each of these parts can follow the user's preferences. We evaluate on the test set of a benchmark with 320 instances in ten room types, with a simulated user and a render-based evaluator. PreferenceScene matches the hidden preferences better than five published text-to-scene baselines adapted to the same interaction, and its match rises with every turn. It even surpasses them when they are given the preferences directly, and a vision-language model rates its scenes as the most realistic and functional.

  • Abstract: We show that quantization asymmetry concentrates bit-flip vulnerability in small tensors retained at high precision. Title: Rethinking Reasoning Effort Training in Large Language Models: On-Policy Distillation or Reinforcement Learning?

  • Authors: Tianliang Lv, Hao Li, Xinchao Xu, Zhengkun Zhang, Ju Huo
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32131
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32131
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32131]] Rethinking Reasoning Effort Training in Large Language Models: On-Policy Distillation or Reinforcement Learning?(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32131)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models have shown strong capabilities on complex tasks, yet how to control their reasoning effort remains a significant challenge for practical deployment. This paper provides a systematic investigation of reasoning-effort training, contrasting reinforcement learning (RL) with on-policy distillation (OPD). We find that compressing reasoning and separating effort tiers are governed by different signals. Length-penalized RL reliably compresses a single policy, reducing thinking on AIME25 by 70\% without accuracy loss, but collapses the tiers when they are trained jointly. Multi-teacher OPD separates them: across 33 benchmarks, low and medium use 58\% and 43\% fewer thinking tokens than high, while high improves on the reference policy by 3.1 points. We trace this difference to the training signal. A sequence-level reward cannot tell tier-specific behavior from shared behavior, whereas OPD supplies a tier-specific target at every visited state, including the decision to stop. Building on this analysis, we show that a single token-level KL anchor on the high tier is sufficient to learn separated tiers within one RL stage, and that natural-language effort instructions make this separation emerge from the start of training, yielding a policy competitive with multi-teacher OPD at matched thinking length. Finally, the separation built by dense targets holds only on the states seen in training: it does not carry over to unseen agentic tasks, where effort acts mainly through the number of interaction turns, whereas the compression induced by sequence-level rewards does.

Title: Encrypted Fixed-Point Multiplication in Quasilinear Complexity

  • Authors: Jung Hee Cheon, Junho Lee
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32157
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32157
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32157]] Encrypted Fixed-Point Multiplication in Quasilinear Complexity(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32157)
  • Keywords: security
  • Abstract: Homomorphic encryption enables computation on encrypted data, but its high computational overhead remains a central obstacle. We overcome this obstacle asymptotically for high-precision fixed-point arithmetic. In this paper, we introduce focused encoding, which represents a fixed-point number of $n$-bit precision using a polynomial of degree $O(n/\log n)$ over a polynomially bounded modulus by concentrating precision at one embedding. We then introduce an error-shaping technique for homomorphic operations to preserve the encoding. This yields an encrypted fixed-point multiplication algorithm for the CKKS scheme that runs in online time $O(n \log n)$ at $n$-bit precision for any fixed multiplicative depth, with security parameter $\lambda = O(n/\log^2 n)$. Further, we develop bootstrapping for focused encoding to overcome the quadratic cost of conventional bootstrapping. We bootstrap ciphertext coefficients at an enlarged scale and shape the resulting error to preserve focused encoding, at the cost of additional modulus consumption. Then, to reduce the remaining logarithmic overhead, we decompose the ciphertext into components over a smaller subring and bootstrap each separately. This reduces the online cost per multiplication, including bootstrapping, to $O(n \log n + n (\log \lambda)^3 \log \log \lambda)$ for security parameter $\lambda = O(n/\log^3 n)$.

Title: A Unified Control Framework for Gradient Inversion Defense in Federated Learning

  • Authors: Lianhai Ren, Chi Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.DC
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32174
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32174
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32174]] A Unified Control Framework for Gradient Inversion Defense in Federated Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32174)
  • Keywords: privacy, protect, defense, attack, federate
  • Abstract: Gradient perturbations are widely recognized as an effective defense against gradient inversion attacks in federated learning, but they are equally known to impair optimization, creating a persistent tension between privacy and utility. In this paper, we show that existing gradient perturbation defenses, despite their different formulations, can be unified under a controlled dynamics framework, providing a cohesive understanding of their optimization behavior and enabling the derivation of universal convergence and safety conditions without relying on case-specific analyses. Leveraging this observation, we propose ``Near Orthogonal Gradient Projection (NODE)'', which injects perturbations only along directions orthogonal to the original gradient so that the defended gradient maintains a fixed cosine similarity with the true gradient. By preserving the first-order descent direction, NODE substantially reduces the optimization cost of strong perturbations while maintaining effective protection against gradient inversion attacks. We theoretically establish convergence guarantees under standard optimization assumptions and further show that, under mild conditions on the gradient distribution, NODE remains effective in preventing inversion attacks. Comprehensive experiments on multiple benchmark datasets, including CIFAR-10, ImageNet, and Chest X-ray, together with a variety of gradient inversion attacks, demonstrate that the proposed framework achieves an improved privacy--utility trade-off over existing defenses across a broad range of training settings.

  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Scaling inference computation offers a way to improve diffusion transformers (DiTs), but taking more sampling steps leaves the depth of each denoising prediction unchanged. Retrofitting pretrained models enables this capability without repeating pretraining, but requires blocks learned for fixed-depth computation to refine states produced by their own repeated application. The challenge is to use each additional pass to correct local errors without disrupting the structure established by earlier passes. We introduce LoopFit, which uses feedback from earlier passes to guide refinement at each image patch. Residual Attention Modulation (RAM) uses each patch's feature change in one pass to scale and shift its input to attention in the next. During training, Residual Relay Loss (RRL) gives greater weight to patches whose prediction errors persist across passes. A noise-dependent depth schedule speeds up sampling by using four passes at high noise and one at lower noise levels. At comparable cost, backbones trained with four passes solve more problems using recurrent depth than extra sampling steps. Across 12 visual puzzle settings, LoopFit solves 89.8% of boards, compared with 87.8% for the strongest baseline evaluated on all 12. Most of the gain comes from tracing long paths on mazes and frozen lakes.

Title: LLM Agent Authorization Across Context Compaction

  • Authors: Zeming Fei, Hongming Fei, Yi Xu, Xiaoyang Wang, Ying Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32194
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32194
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32194]] LLM Agent Authorization Across Context Compaction(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32194)
  • Keywords: security, protect, attack
  • Abstract: Request-derived authorizers such as Progent protect LLM agents from prompt injection by deriving what an agent may do from the user's request. Their guarantee rests on attribution: the request is taken to be what the user said. Compaction breaks it. At every automatic compaction we observed, Claude Code and Codex placed a model-written summary of earlier turns as a user message, so the compactor's quotation of the user, documents and injected text reads as the user speaking. Because a policy frozen at the first request lets the agent finish few later tasks, we let Progent derive its policy for each request, and find that what it grants then follows what the compactor wrote. Even a payee the user stated is not safe: when an attacker's notice states another account as plain data and the user asks to pay the rent, the policy derived from a frontier compactor's summary admits the notice's account in 91% of cases, and the one derived from the user's own words in none. The cause is general: we prove that an authorizer that cannot see who wrote which bytes cannot treat a forged record differently from the legitimate one it imitates by more than the two are distinguishable, and that the bound is tight. Frontier compactors bear this out: they mark injected instructions, which have no legitimate twin, but rarely injected data, which does. What a compactor keeps, and how it marks it, is a security parameter. The fix follows from attribution: the authorizer credits only what the user said, authenticated on arrival and carried verbatim across the boundary, while the agent still reads the summary. We prove that it then receives exactly that text or nothing, so an attacker can block the policy but cannot shape it, and adaptive attacks on our implementation confirm it. In 40 attacked AgentDojo episodes re-run with the fix, no attacker action completes while the agent reads the summary.

Title: When Answering Is Administering: Permission Replies That Rewrite Coding-Agent Policy

  • Authors: Zeming Fei, Hongming Fei, Jingwen Chen, Xiaoyang Wang, Prosanta Gope, Biplab Sikdar, Ying Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32195
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32195
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32195]] When Answering Is Administering: Permission Replies That Rewrite Coding-Agent Policy(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32195)
  • Keywords: protect, attack
  • Abstract: Coding agents let plugins and remote services answer permission prompts, and Claude Code documents that such an answer cannot override the operator's deny rules. However, we show that this guarantee, checked one decision at a time, fails over a whole run, because an answer to one permission prompt can rewrite the policy that every later answer is checked against. For example, a remote service with no access to the operator's files approves an unrelated command and attaches an update that deletes the deny rule protecting the project's secrets. The agent then reads the secrets in that session and the next, even inside the vendor's strict sandbox. The root cause is that Claude Code checks only updates that add or replace allow rules. Relays that forward such deletions already exist, including the vendor's own Remote Control. In effect, the right to answer a prompt has become the right to administer the policy. Worse, the deletion leaves the same records as a local edit by the operator, so Claude Code cannot tell who wrote a rule. More generally, what a runtime records decides what it can safely delegate. A runtime that cannot tell who wrote a rule can accept only updates that keep every currently denied call denied, whereas, to protect every party's restrictions, a runtime that records who wrote each rule can let each party remove only the restrictions proven to be its own. We prove that these two bounds are tight. They correctly predict whether the operator's restrictions survive in all thirteen permission designs across ten agent systems, including predictions we wrote down before running Codex, Gemini CLI, opencode, and Goose. Enforcing the bounds at the shared update step blocks the attack. A survey of 2,227 fetched marketplace plugins finds only two that send policy updates.

Title: Steerable by Design: Conditional Pretraining for Robust Control of Language Models

  • Authors: Henry Castillo, Yanchen Liu, Claudio Mayrink Verdun, Dylan Hadfield-Menell
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32199
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32199
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32199]] Steerable by Design: Conditional Pretraining for Robust Control of Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32199)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Our goal is to build language models that are steerable by design. Today's language models are pretrained with next-token prediction, which relegates alignment and control to the final 1% of training tokens or inference-time interventions. These models repurpose a single input channel for both instructions and data, leading to common failure modes where the model mistakenly treats data as instructions such as intentional jailbreaks and unintentional in-context alignment drift. Mancelle-3B steers more strongly than prompt- and activation-based methods on comparable open models while maintaining fluency, and it withstands adversarial black-box and soft-prompt counter-steering that defeats those baselines. Title: Who gets the information? An $α$-fair label-coverage framework for multi-label feature selection

  • Authors: Zhenzhi Tang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32200
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32200
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32200]] Who gets the information? An $α$-fair label-coverage framework for multi-label feature selection(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32200)
  • Keywords: fair
  • Abstract: Recent information-theoretic filters for multi-label feature selection disagree on how to distribute relevance over labels: some amplify features that inform a few labels, others balance what every label receives. We show these choices are implicit social welfare functions. Treating labels as agents whose utility is the information they receive, we propose FLIC, which greedily maximises the $\alpha$-fair welfare of per-label information coverage. A single parameter $\alpha$ moves from utilitarian ($\alpha=0$) through proportionally fair ($\alpha=1$) to max-min selection ($\alpha\to\infty$), and a dozen published relevance terms lie on this axis, most as special cases, first-order approximations or limits. The objective is monotone submodular for every $\alpha\ge0$ whenever each label's information is, so greedy selection is a $(1-1/e)$-approximation; this fails for $\alpha<0$, and the max-min limit is inapproximable. Fano's and the Hellman-Raviv inequalities tie raw coverage to the Bayes Hamming loss and normalised coverage to per-label relative error. FLIC uses bias-corrected, label-wise conditional information and has no relevance-redundancy trade-off parameter. On 22 data sets, FLIC is the best of 14 information-theoretic filters on seven of nine metrics, winning 86 of 117 pairwise comparisons significantly and losing none significantly. Among 17 methods it ranks first on ranking loss, coverage and macro-AUC with two classifiers and has the best mean rank (narrowly with ML-kNN), while three embedded methods lead on Hamming loss, one-error and micro-F1.

  • Abstract: 3D Gaussian splats enable real-time rendering but require substantial storage. We exploit this freedom on the decoding side with SplatTok, a learned scene-resynthesis codec. Trained across thousands of real-world scenes, a transformer encodes an existing splat into compact, spatially anchored latent tokens, and a shared decoder synthesizes a new set of standard Gaussian primitives without preserving correspondence to the input. Title: Joint Classification and Generation on Tabular Data: an Empirical Study

  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Many applications of machine learning on tabular data need both a classifier and a generator of realistic records. The default is to train two separate models, whereas a single joint model promises both from one set of weights. Several families of tabular deep learning models can be turned into such a joint model, which raises the question of which joint model is preferable at which computational budget. In this empirical study, we introduce and compare joint models based on four state-of-the-art tabular data modeling families (energy-based models, probabilistic circuits, tabular foundation models and tabular diffusion models) against measured FLOPs and wall-clock time, on 29 classification datasets. We find that the best joint model is a probabilistic circuit at the smallest budgets and, beyond that, a tabular foundation model that generates column by column using a regressor (TabICL). Compared with pairs of separate models, this study finds a joint model is rarely preferable but often very close to the best pair, without the hassle of training and maintaining two separate models.

Title: Relational Bottleneck Models

  • Authors: Shin'ya Yamaguchi, Kosuke Nishida
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32227
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32227
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32227]] Relational Bottleneck Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32227)
  • Keywords: interpretability
  • Abstract: Achieving both interpretable and high-performance inference is a key challenge in deep learning. While Concept Bottleneck Models (CBMs) provide inherent interpretability, they are constrained by task-specific training and rely on isolated visual concepts that fail to capture higher-order visual semantic relationships. Conversely, Large Vision Language Models (LVLMs) demonstrate zero-shot capabilities through Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning. However, standard CoT lacks a strict causal dependency between the generated rationale and the final output, compromising faithfulness. To bridge this gap, we propose Relational Bottleneck Models (RBMs), a novel training-free framework that transforms any off-the-shelf LVLMs into inherently interpretable models in a plug-and-play manner. RBMs consist of two stages: (i) Structured Rationale Generation (SRG), which explicitly models semantic interactions between visual concepts as subject-predicate-object triplets, and (ii) Rationale-Grounded Decoding (RGD), which enforces a strict causal dependency between the rationales and the final output. RGD aggregates rationale-conditional next-token distributions via an importance-weighted product-of-experts strategy, strictly excluding the image inputs from the decoding context. Extensive zero-shot evaluations across diverse benchmarks and LVLM backbones demonstrate that RBMs achieve superior interpretability while improving task performance over CBM baselines, entirely without task-specific fine-tuning.

Title: MYSTIQUE: Few-Shot Facial Identity Transfer with Performance Preservation

  • Authors: Shihan Cheng, David Hyde, Junting Deng, Michael Bao, Paul Debevec, Roshni Cooper, Soshi Shimada
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.GR, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32232
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32232
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32232]] MYSTIQUE: Few-Shot Facial Identity Transfer with Performance Preservation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32232)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: In visual effects, identity transfer replaces the identity in a driving image with that of a target person, while preserving the expression, lighting, pose and scene of that image. This shortcut causes identity leakage at inference, where the driving image shows a different person. Our key observation is that leakage can enter through any input that reads the driving image. We therefore present MYSTIQUE, a diffusion-based identity transfer method that takes identity from a set of the target's photos and reads expression, lighting and scene through separate streams. At inference, we also warp the lighting input onto the face shape of a first pass, which reduces face-shape leakage through the lighting stream. Since face-recognition scores and human judgments each have limits, we evaluate identity with both, together with face-shape metrics on rendered faces of known shape. In a user study, raters judge MYSTIQUE's results more like the target than those of each compared method. Among these methods, MYSTIQUE also recovers the target's face shape most accurately, and its recognition score gains the most as more photos of the target are given.

Title: CREDIT: Compositional Reliability Learning for Noisy Composed Image Retrieval

  • Authors: Tiancai Zhou, Yongxiang Li, Suimei Liu, Zhong Yuan, Dezhong Peng, Xi Peng, Xu Wang
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32310
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32310
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32310]] CREDIT: Compositional Reliability Learning for Noisy Composed Image Retrieval(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32310)
  • Keywords: robust, diffusion
  • Abstract: Composed Image Retrieval (CIR) aims to retrieve a target image that satisfies user intent based on a multimodal query consisting of a reference image and a modification text. However, real-world training data are often corrupted by Noisy Triplet Correspondence (NTC), where the semantic correspondence among the reference image, modification text, and target image becomes unreliable. Existing methods implicitly assume that the correctness of triplet correspondence can be determined by fixed decision rules. However, under NTC, the authenticity of semantic correspondence is difficult to determine accurately, and different triplets exhibit varying degrees of semantic reliability rather than binary correctness. Consequently, deterministic confidence is insufficient to faithfully characterize correspondence reliability, often leading to overconfident supervision for unreliable triplets and progressively reinforcing noisy semantics during compositional representation learning. To address this challenge, we propose a Compositional Reliability learning framework with Evidential uncertainty and DiffusIon-guided refinemenT (CREDIT), which explicitly quantifies the uncertainty of triplet correspondence to estimate its reliability and suppress the propagation of noisy supervision. CREDIT consists of two complementary components: The Evidential Compositional Uncertainty Learning (ECUL) module explicitly models correspondence uncertainty via evidential learning, enabling uncertaintyaware reliability estimation to suppress noisy supervision. Building upon the reliable supervision provided by ECUL, the Diffusion-enhanced Reference Refinement (DRR) module further refines reference representations through textconditioned diffusion, producing more robust compositional query features. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CREDIT consistently outperforms existing methods under various settings.

Title: ASTERISM: Do Multimodal LLMs Actually Look at All the Images?

  • Authors: Chanseul Cho, Dongwoo Kim, Noori Bae, Seungjae Moon, Seongsu Lee, Youngmin Ro
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32316
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32316
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32316]] ASTERISM: Do Multimodal LLMs Actually Look at All the Images?(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32316)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Multi-image benchmarks aim to test whether a multimodal large language model (MLLM) combines evidence across images, but many of their items can be answered from a single image. Across about 33k items from 19 public benchmarks, our audit and a two-stage single-image filter find that one image or the question alone settles 24.2% of them. On these items, MLLMs recover from one image a median 78% of the accuracy that all images add over the text alone, so these items reward answering from one image, which we call the single-image shortcut. MLLMs take this shortcut even on items where one image is not enough. On such items, most of their wrong answers repeat an answer they give from one image, at a rate well above chance. We build ASTERISM, to our knowledge the largest audited multi-image benchmark, from these 19 benchmarks by keeping their items that need more than one image. Its 18.7k items, 2.0k of them corrected or rewritten, share one format, one scorer and seven task categories. We also release ASTERISM-Train with 49k training items and ASTERISM-Lite, a hard subset whose items we manually verify one by one. On ASTERISM-Lite, the strongest proprietary MLLM scores 68%. Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), now common in MLLM post-training, rewards the shortcut as well, because a correct answer read off one image earns as much reward as one that combines images. In line with this, existing RLVR objectives add little to the ASTERISM score. To stop rewarding the shortcut in training as ASTERISM does in evaluation, we propose cross-image credit assignment (CICA), an extension of RLVR that moves the credit toward the tokens that need more than one image. On three open-source MLLMs, CICA raises the ASTERISM score by 2.6 to 6.0 points, more than twice the gain of each general and vision-specific RL objective trained on the same data.

Title: LRQ-Cache: Compressing Feature Caches in Video Diffusion via Low-Rank Approximation and Quantization

  • Authors: Ruizhe Yao, Zhen Huang, Songpan Gao, Danyi Liu, Zijian Cao, Weijie Zheng, Haohuan Fu
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32345
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32345
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32345]] LRQ-Cache: Compressing Feature Caches in Video Diffusion via Low-Rank Approximation and Quantization(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32345)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Video diffusion transformers (DiTs) achieve high-fidelity generation, but inference is expensive because every denoising step evaluates a large transformer over all spatiotemporal tokens of the video. Temporal caching accelerates them by reusing intermediate features across denoising steps. However, retaining dense feature tensors introduces a persistent storage cost that grows with video length, resolution, and cache coverage, reaching 109-346 GiB per video for the predictive caches we evaluate. Such states exceed the memory of a single GPU, so native caches must either be offloaded to the CPU, which slows generation, or be sharded across several GPUs, e.g., with sequence parallelism, so that the cache rather than the compute sets the number of GPUs. We instead shrink the cached state itself, exploiting its redundancy: a few channel patterns shared across tokens capture much of the energy of each cached feature. Based on this observation, we propose LRQ-Cache, a training-free method that combines low-rank approximation and quantization for feature-cache compression. LRQ-Cache represents each cached feature with a small set of factors, quantizes them before storage, and reconstructs the feature at reuse time, leaving cache mechanisms unchanged. Across three caching mechanisms on HunyuanVideo and Wan2.1, LRQ-Cache reduces the persistent state of predictive caches by 42-71x, to 2.3-4.9 GiB per video. VBench stays within 0.25 pp of the native cache for TeaCache, and the larger drops of the Wan2.1 predictive caches, up to 3.5 pp, shrink at higher ranks. As a result, predictive caches that exceed the memory of a single 80 GB H100 GPU fit on it with LRQ-Cache, making generation 2.7-5.9x faster than with CPU-offloaded native caches.

Title: Beyond Hard Tokens: Predictive Memory for Multimodal Protein Generation

  • Authors: Xiaoliang Shi, Zichen Wang, Zhijian Wei, Zhanpeng Shi, Zhenghong Zhou, Yunpeng Wang, Shuangjia Zheng

  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32347]] Beyond Hard Tokens: Predictive Memory for Multimodal Protein Generation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32347)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Multimodal protein language models unify sequence and discrete structural tokens via iterative masked decoding, offering a promising paradigm for joint protein design. However, standard decoders rely on a memoryless mask reset at each iteration: only a sparse subset of newly committed hard tokens is carried forward, while all remaining unresolved sites are reset to an identical [MASK] embedding. In open-ended co-generation where neither modality provides a fixed external anchor, this stateless transition limits single-step refinement depth and deprives both tracks of stable cross-step soft anchors, leading to slow convergence or degenerative repetitive collapse. Observing that uncommitted predictions evolve with pronounced temporal coherence rather than erratic noise, we introduce Predictive Memory (PredMem), a training-free self-conditioning recurrence that carries these soft beliefs across steps. At each step, PredMem summarizes raw logits into a probability-weighted Top-$k$ expectation embedding at every residue and affinely interpolates it into eligible masked inputs on the next step at zero extra denoiser cost. Controlled diagnostics show that these gains stem primarily from early trajectory stabilization in unanchored cross-modal co-evolution rather than extra compute or simple entropy reduction, establishing PredMem as an efficient inference mechanism for multimodal discrete generation.

Title: Sub-Riemannian structure for Oriented Point Clouds

  • Authors: Thomas Besnier, Stefan Sommer
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32361
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32361

  • Keywords: diffusion, segmentation
  • Abstract: Many frameworks dealing with 3D (oriented) point clouds define locality in the ambient Euclidean space: $k$-nearest-neighbor graphs and radius balls in $\mathbb{R}^3$ are critical to define which points are "close to one another" and should exchange information. This can unintentionally merge sampled surfaces that are close in space but geometrically distinct such as the two faces of a thin leaf. Then, features are more prone to leak across boundaries which hinder performance. To address this, we propose to define a sub-Riemannian contact metric on position-orientation space $\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{R}^3\times \mathbb{S}^2$: points are lifted to $(x,n)$ with $n$ a surface normal, and "motion" is constrained to a non-holonomic horizontal distribution in which spatial velocity must stay in the plane orthogonal to the normal which can reorient at a cost. From this, we derive an explicit score, which locally approximate the distance, to compare oriented points for downstream tasks. We report improved segmentation and correspondence accuracy near contact when using the score as a drop-in change inside PointNet++ aggregation mechanism as well as reduced leakage when using it to simulate heat diffusion on oriented points.

Title: The Activity Geometry of Discrete Generative Sampling

  • Authors: Xinlong Guo, Jie Zhang, Lingyou Zhou
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32380
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32380
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32380]] The Activity Geometry of Discrete Generative Sampling(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32380)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Iterative refinement is a key capability of diffusion-based generation and has motivated growing efforts to bring similar behavior to discrete diffusion and flow models. A growing line of work enables such refinement by allowing previously generated tokens to be revised during sampling, thereby shaping the coupling between successive sampling states. Despite its growing role in sampler design, this coupling freedom remains insufficiently characterized. We introduce Activity Geometry to study these finite-step couplings through expected transition cost, which we call activity. We show that activity decomposes into the minimum required by the induced marginal transport and an excess term arising from the coupling. Using this minimum as a reference, we propose Minimal-Activity Finite-Time Coupling (MAFTC) to realize prescribed marginals with minimum activity without retraining or additional model evaluations. In the continuous-time limit, the minimum activity per unit time converges to the minimum expected jump rate compatible with the prescribed path. For metric-induced Gibbs paths, we further show that the activity of the kinetic-optimal flux admits a geometric decomposition. We find that total activity alone can obscure coupling excess: a sampler may approach the minimum activity required by the prescribed marginal transport while still retaining substantial coupling excess. In language generation, as the number of model evaluations increases, refinement-based samplers accumulate additional trajectory activity with limited diversity gains, whereas MAFTC reduces trajectory activity while maintaining comparable generation quality and higher lexical diversity.

Title: The Teacher Is Already Inside: Self-Distilling Looped Language Models Beyond Their Last Loop

  • Authors: Linghua Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32452
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32452
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32452]] The Teacher Is Already Inside: Self-Distilling Looped Language Models Beyond Their Last Loop(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32452)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Looped language models apply one shared transformer stack several times per token, and every loop is readable through the shared language-model head. Post-training them is hard. Reward-only reinforcement learning needs a verifier and learns from one sparse signal per response; spreading the reward over loops collapses the entropy of every loop; and cross-loop self-distillation, as in LoopOPD, can only teach intermediate loops, because the last loop cannot learn from its own frozen copy: the loss is zero at initialisation. We observe that a looped model already contains a policy that decodes better than its last loop: the next-token distribution extrapolated from the first loop through the last. ELPSD (Extrapolated-Loop Policy Self-Distillation) turns this distribution into a self-distillation target: a frozen copy of the model computes it, and the model's own last loop is distilled towards it on its own rollouts, using the exact reverse KL at every visited prefix. Teacher and student are the same model, so ELPSD needs no reward, no external teacher and no privileged context, and it leaves rollouts and decoding unchanged. We show that the objective pulls the last loop towards the base model's last loop with weight 1+w and away from the base model's first loop with weight w, and that the teacher must stay frozen, since an idealised teacher recomputed from the current model extrapolates without bound. We evaluate ELPSD on Ouro-1.4B-Thinking across five math benchmarks against the base model, GRPO, RLTT and OPSD. Without any reward, ELPSD raises the average avg@8 from 61.15 to 63.69 and is the best method on all five benchmarks, ahead of GRPO (62.32), which uses a verifier, and the self-distillation method OPSD (61.77), which needs reference solutions.

  • Abstract: Action-conditioned world models play a vital role in robotics and autonomous driving, yet existing video diffusion models struggle to maintain dynamic object persistence. To resolve this issue, we propose OmniPersist, a unified framework that couples perspective video generation with panoramic evolution within a single-stage diffusion model, without relying on auxiliary models or initial panoramic inputs. To maintain efficiency, we model the panoramic stream at a lower frame rate, bridging both views across space and time via Geometric Anchoring (GeoAnch) for spatial correspondence and Temporal Compensation (TemComp) for temporal aggregation. OmniPersist is agnostic to camera-control architectures, supporting both latent-level and attention-level conditioning. Extensive experiments on a newly constructed dynamic benchmark across image-to-video and video continuation tasks demonstrate consistent gains in visual quality, camera controllability, revisit consistency, and dynamic persistence.

Title: Progressive Reliable Supervision for Black-Box Universal Domain Adaptation

  • Authors: Jing Li, Zhe Zhang, Huateng Sun, Yuhui Yang, Wanli Xue, Xu Cheng, Fan Shi, Jianhua Zhang, Qinghua Hu, Shengyong Chen
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32474
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32474
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32474]] Progressive Reliable Supervision for Black-Box Universal Domain Adaptation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32474)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Black-Box Universal Domain Adaptation (B$^2$-UniDA) aims to adapt a target model to an unlabeled target domain under arbitrary label-space relationships, while neither source data nor source-model parameters are accessible during adaptation. Under such restrictive information access, reliable target supervision is unavailable, while predictions from the black-box source model can be unreliable due to source bias and domain shifts. To address this challenge, we propose Progressive Reliable Supervision (PReS), which progressively establishes and refines reliable target supervision by coordinating complementary knowledge from the black-box source model, foundation models, and the evolving target model. PReS first identifies reliable known samples by exploiting semantic consistency under diffusion-based perturbations and cross-model consensus between the black-box source model and CLIP. It then estimates the sample-wise reliability of the two predictors and adaptively integrates their complementary predictions to construct reliable pseudo labels. The resulting supervision is exploited asymmetrically to encourage confident and discriminative predictions for reliable known samples while maintaining uncertain yet locally consistent predictions for potential unknown samples. As adaptation proceeds, the emerging target-domain knowledge is further leveraged to refine both the pseudo labels and the known/unknown sample partition, forming a closed-loop process in which supervision construction and target adaptation mutually reinforce each other. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmark datasets under diverse label-space relationships demonstrate that PReS achieves state-of-the-art or highly competitive performance under the restrictive B$^2$-UniDA setting.

Title: A Star is Reborn: The PHP Interpreter Rewritten in Safe Rust

  • Authors: Tianyu Li, Bo Wang, Prateek Saxena, Umang Mathur
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32477
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32477
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32477]] A Star is Reborn: The PHP Interpreter Rewritten in Safe Rust(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32477)
  • Keywords: security
  • Abstract: PHP powers most of the web, yet its C interpreter has had many memory-safety vulnerabilities. Reimplementing the interpreter in safe Rust offers a promising way to eliminate these vulnerabilities. However, eliminating native memory violations alone does not necessarily prevent unintended accesses to the logical memory of the translated program. To prevent such failures, we introduce logical memory safety, which confines the program operations to their permitted memory. In this paper, we formalize this property for PHP through an abstract PHP machine model that captures how the PHP interpreter manages program values while abstracting away functionality details. Guided by this model, we reimplement the PHP 5.0.0 engine in safe Rust, and formally prove that, for a core fragment of PHP, our Rust translation refines the abstract PHP machine, thereby establishing logical memory safety for this fragment. Our translation safely handles all 173 memory vulnerabilities we collected that affect PHP 5.0.0 prior to the rewrite. The Rust version matches the C implementation on all functionality tests, and produces identical outputs for the real-world PHP applications we evaluate. To our knowledge, this is the first principled safe Rust port of a core fragment of the PHP interpreter that combines formal guarantees of logical memory safety with functional compatibility. The improved security comes at a modest average runtime overhead of only 15% compared to C.

Title: Déjà-VAE: Training-Free Consistent Editing with Pseudo-Invertible Encoders and Latent Back-Projection

  • Authors: Ron Libman, Yamit Ehrlich, Ohad Fried, Assaf Shocher
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32501
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32501
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32501]] Déjà-VAE: Training-Free Consistent Editing with Pseudo-Invertible Encoders and Latent Back-Projection(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32501)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Consistency is essential to generative workflows: repeated processing should preserve information beyond the changes explicitly requested. In latent diffusion models, this requires stability both when moving between latent and pixel spaces and when applying successive edits. Autoencoding errors and unintended changes from the editing model can accumulate across these operations, progressively altering image structure, texture, and color. We introduce Déjà-VAE, a framework that combines exact autoencoding consistency with calibrated editing stabilization, without training or fine-tuning. Its autoencoding component converts a pretrained variational autoencoder into a surjective pseudo-invertible neural network, with a fixed gain balancing deviations from the pretrained encoder and decoder. The modified decoder $D'$ is an explicit nonlinear right inverse of the encoder $E'$, satisfying $E'(D'(z))=z$ by construction. Consequently, $D'$ is a reflexive generalized inverse: latent round trips preserve their input, up to numerical precision, and repeated image reconstruction is idempotent. To suppress editing drift, adaptive wavelet back-projection finds the closest latent to the editor's output in $\ell_2$, subject to matching the previous latent on wavelet coefficients whose changes fall below calibrated thresholds. This correction is applied once per turn, with no additional network evaluations after calibration. Together, these components support workflows that remain in latent space or interleave generation with pixel-space operations and constraints. Tests across models and editing benchmarks show less drift than stock pipelines and dedicated consistency methods.

Title: DiffAnchor: Anchor-Space Diffusion for Monocular 3D Hand Pose Estimation

  • Authors: Changlong Jiang, Youci Tang, Haohong Kuang, Hang Xu, Jinghong Zheng, Ran Wang, Zhenghong Yu, Chen Feng, Xiaofeng Yue, Yang Xiao
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32513
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32513
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32513]] DiffAnchor: Anchor-Space Diffusion for Monocular 3D Hand Pose Estimation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32513)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Accurate and efficient monocular 3D hand pose estimation is an important perception capability for embodied human interaction and dexterous manipulation. Recent diffusion-based methods model pose uncertainty by iteratively denoising 3D joint coordinates. However, directly diffusing the final joints requires the generative process to simultaneously explore plausible poses and recover precise coordinates, often relying on additional denoising steps or multiple hypotheses and consequently increasing inference cost. In this work, we propose DiffAnchor, a few-step anchor-space diffusion framework that diffuses anchors rather than joints. Our key insight is that stochastic exploration does not need to operate directly on the final output. This design reduces the precision burden of the diffusion process and enables accurate prediction using only a few denoising steps and a single hypothesis. Extensive experiments on HO-3D V2, NYU, ICVL, and InterHand2.6M demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on the first three benchmarks and competitive performance on challenging interacting-hand estimation, while maintaining inference efficiency comparable to deterministic regression baselines.

Title: Smooth Essential-Manifold Residuals for View-Graph Calibration

  • Authors: Viktor Larsson, Johannes Schönberger
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32540
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32540
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32540]] Smooth Essential-Manifold Residuals for View-Graph Calibration(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32540)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: View-graph calibration jointly estimates camera intrinsics from multiple pairwise fundamental matrices. The correct intrinsics transform each fundamental matrix into an essential matrix, casting the calibration problem as minimizing the distance to the essential manifold. We study how to represent this objective for nonlinear least squares. The conventional singular-value formulation collapses two independent essential-matrix constraints into one scalar residual. Its per-edge Gauss-Newton model is therefore rank deficient, which leads to poor convergence. We instead derive an isometric reduction to 2x2 matrices, where each branch of the nonlinear essential-matrix constraint becomes a linear subspace. This reduction yields a smooth two-dimensional residual whose squared norm is exactly the original manifold distance, whose Gauss-Newton approximation is generically full rank, and whose evaluation requires no per-edge singular value decomposition. Experiments show that the new residual retains the estimation accuracy of the geometric objective while converging in fewer, computationally cheaper iterations. Combined with robust initialization and optimization, it achieves state-of-the-art view-graph calibration accuracy at a runtime comparable to inexpensive algebraic objectives.

  • Abstract: Algorithmic recourse provides actionable recommendations that enable individuals to achieve favourable outcomes.   • Abstract: Real-world generative problems in biology, medical imaging, or robotics, rarely come with perfectly paired source and target distributions. Title: MinPair: Measured Minimal Pairs for Fine-Grained Control in Text-to-Motion Generation

  • Authors: Lennart Schiweck, Cristóbal Curio
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32549
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32549
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32549]] MinPair: Measured Minimal Pairs for Fine-Grained Control in Text-to-Motion Generation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32549)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Text-to-motion generation has made great progress in capturing what action to perform, yet it remains limited in controlling how that action is executed. For example, when only the height word of a kick request changes, none of the tested state-of-the-art methods reaches all three of knee, waist and head height. The other words of a caption usually imply an attribute such as height, so neither the training loss nor the standard metrics require a model to use the word that states it. A model has to use this word only when it alone separates two otherwise identical captions. We introduce MinPair, which creates such caption pairs from real motion clips. It starts from rule-based motion descriptors that measure the 3D body motion of each clip. From these descriptors a language model writes MinPair captions, timed body-part captions that say how each body part moves. The same descriptors show in which attributes two clips of the same action differ. A measured minimal pair joins the true caption of one clip with an altered copy in which only one attribute takes the value measured in the other clip. A contrastive pair loss then requires a diffusion denoiser to reconstruct the clip more accurately from its true caption than from the altered copy. Training needs no synthetic motion and inference stays unchanged. Control is tested on 22 target tasks and on caption edits that each change one attribute. MinPair captions alone help motions reach their targets and improve the standard metrics M2M, R@3 and FID, yet a model trained on them still ignores most single-word edits. The pair loss raises the share of followed template edits from 11% to 60%, while the standard metrics do not improve further. With body-part captions, MinPair reaches more targets than state-of-the-art methods that were trained on up to 99 times more motion data.

Pretrained 3D experts provide spatially grounded region proposals, while 2D experts provide semantic cues for identifying object parts. The challenge is to integrate these complementary capabilities into a framework that can both produce meaningful part segmentations and progressively refine them. We present PartAgent, a training-free agentic framework for 3D part segmentation built upon two complementary modules: (i) Structural-Semantic Expert Integration combines regions predicted by the 3D expert with multiview semantic evidence from the 2D expert through image-to-surface correspondences, constructing compositional part partitions at multiple granularities. (ii) Observation-Driven Closed-Loop Partition Refinement observes the current partition and its supporting evidence to select targeted inspection, evidence repair, composition, or reselection actions. It verifies proposed changes and uses the updated state and action outcomes to guide subsequent decisions. On PartObjaverse-Tiny-WT, PartAgent achieves 68.41 mIoU, outperforming the strongest competing method by 8.53 points. Across 3DCoMPaT200, PartNeXt, PartNetE, and PartObjaverse-Tiny, PartAgent achieves the highest mIoU on each dataset, averaging 69.36 across the four datasets, 7.28 points above the highest mean among competing methods.

Title: Sol$^{3}$: Accelerating Video Diffusion for Free via Hierarchical Sparse Attention Routing

  • Authors: Guoxin Fan, Weilun Feng, Chuanguang Yang, Mingqiang Wu, Xiaokun Liu, Jitao Lu, Zhulin An, Libo Huang, Boyu Diao, Di Gao, Yongjun Xu
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32562
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32562
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32562]] Sol$^{3}$: Accelerating Video Diffusion for Free via Hierarchical Sparse Attention Routing(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32562)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Sol-Attn (Sparsifying online attention) combines sparse exact computation with low-cost representative approximation to accelerate training-free inference in diffusion video models. However, intra-block attention can focus on only a few tokens. While preserving these key interactions exactly, the block-level decision incurs the cost of evaluating many low-response members. We propose Sol$^{3}$, a training-free hierarchical sparse attention method that further refines how exact computations are allocated. First, we introduce Gaussian-guided hierarchical routing, which determines the scope of exact computation step by step, from blocks to tokens, using statistical thresholds at each level. Only a subset of members within Mixed blocks that require refinement is selected for exact computation, while representative terms approximate the remaining members. We further design heterogeneous routing statistics that combine block-center correlations with variations in Key and Value within blocks to identify blocks that are difficult to approximate. Among these Mixed blocks, we uniformly compare raw token relevance to select members requiring exact computation. Experiments on video generation, audio-video co-generation, and world simulation tasks demonstrate that Sol$^{3}$ achieves 86% attention sparsity and delivers a $3.07\times$ attention speedup while preserving quality. Our code is available at this https URL.

Title: Evaluating Latent Knowledge Stability in Multimodal Large Language Models

  • Authors: Chuancheng Shi, Shangze Li, Wenhua Wu, Zifeng Cheng, Pengyang Shao, Fei Shen, Tat-Seng Chua

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Changes in image viewpoint and question formulation can alter latent knowledge representations in multimodal large language models (MLLMs). However, output correctness and overall representational drift provide limited insight into how these changes affect model decisions. We therefore evaluate latent knowledge stability across visual and linguistic elicitation conditions from two complementary perspectives: answer preference and internal support. Specifically, we devise the margin retention score (MRS), which measures the first-order projected degradation of the initial answer's logit margin over competing options across conditions and assesses how changes in latent knowledge representations affect this margin. We also present latent knowledge support-structure stability (KSS), which evaluates reference-correct items by comparing internal support profiles constructed using the margin gradient under the reference condition, which jointly reflects changes in how internal support is distributed and changes in total support magnitude. We further construct MMStableBench, comprising 19,452 knowledge items (facts), each probing the same latent knowledge across varied image viewpoints, semantically equivalent question formulations, and answer-option permutations. Experiments across 15 MLLMs show that MRS and KSS reveal latent knowledge instability not captured by accuracy, while an MRS-inspired regularizer further improves the generalization of edited latent knowledge.

  • Authors: Nora A. M. A. Talkhan, Ibrahim Abdelbaky, Fatma A. Omara
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32568
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32568

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Improvements in molecular property prediction are usually attributed to the model, although part of the gain may come from the molecular representation. New models are commonly benchmarked against a random forest (RF) trained on a molecular fingerprint, a comparison that changes the model and the representation at the same time. We separate the two contributions with a two-by-two factorial design in which the RF and a second model (CatBoost or the tabular foundation models TabPFN and TabFM) are each trained on a fingerprint and on RDKit descriptors, and each effect is tested with a paired scaffold bootstrap. For solubility and lipophilicity, the representation effect exceeds the model effect in every main factorial design, in all three evaluations. On the classification datasets, only one main factorial design has a significant difference between the two effects. Although the model effect is smaller on solubility and lipophilicity, TabFM outperforms the RF on all 7 datasets when both use RDKit descriptors. With RDKit descriptors in place of a fingerprint, the RF baseline reported for DGT surpasses the transformer's published solubility score. We recommend that model comparisons evaluate classical baselines on more than one representation and report the representation and model effects separately.

Title: Boiling Down Uncertainty: Deep Generative Scenario Search with Decision-Guided Evolution Strategies for Two-Stage Stochastic Optimization

  • Authors: Atefeh Hemmati Golsefidi, David Pisinger
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, math.OC
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32573
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32573
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32573]] Boiling Down Uncertainty: Deep Generative Scenario Search with Decision-Guided Evolution Strategies for Two-Stage Stochastic Optimization(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32573)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: One of the most common models for handling uncertainty is two-stage stochastic programming. Choosing the right number of scenarios is a trade-off between the uncertainty that can be represented and computational cost. A recent method, Deep Generative Scenario Search, generates synthetic scenarios and improves them by learning from the decisions they induce. The original approach used a black-box gradient estimate, since decisions are piecewise constant across scenarios. This paper provides the missing analytical foundation by deriving the gradient in closed form from recourse duality and an adjoint solve of the regularized reduced problem. The decision-focused direction, with a heuristic direction as a backup when duals are unavailable, defines the sampling subspace of a guided evolution strategy rather than being used as a direct update. For any guiding subspace, the expected update is a descent direction of the smoothed objective, so an inaccurate guide adds variance while the expected step still descends. For six problem families, both guided estimators outperform the baseline. The decision-focused version reduces the mean gap by three-quarters, and on the two families with the most integer variables the reduced sets beat the exact solver's two-hour incumbent in most runs, using a fifth of the time.

Title: Understanding the Importance of Massive Activations through Adversarial Robustness

  • Abstract: A common occurrence in Large Language Models (LLMs) is the emergence of so-called massive activations. However, the reasons and consequences for their appearance are not yet well understood. In this paper, we explain the role of massive activations in deep learning by showing that they are connected to the region migration phenomenon in delayed robustness. Under the spline theoretic interpretation of deep networks, we show that massive activations push the representations away from the nonlinearity boundaries of the network, decreasing the complexity of its input-output map around data points. We consistently observe that massive activations and region migration co-occur with adversarial robustness. Furthermore, we illustrate that this connection holds in the large-scale pretraining of LLMs by showing that the rise of massive activations in LLMs closely tracks the adversarial accuracy of the model under perturbations of the token embeddings.

Title: HiCal: Offline Calibration for Hierarchical Scale Encoding in Accurate HiF4 Quantization

  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32624
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32624

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Block floating-point (BFP) quantization is widely adopted for efficient low-bit large language model (LLM) inference, where the choice of block scales plays a critical role in quantization accuracy. HiFloat4 (HiF4) employs a hierarchical scale encoding scheme consisting of a global E6M2 scale and two levels of binary scale extensions. However, its conventional rounding rule and fixed scale extension thresholds may not fully capture the statistical characteristics of the quantized tensors in practice, leading to suboptimal scale representations. In this work, we propose HiCal, an offline calibration approach that improves HiF4 quantization accuracy by refining the encoding decisions across all three scale levels. Specifically, we introduce a calibrated rounding bias for the Level-1 E6M2 scale and optimize the scale-extension thresholds at Levels 2 and 3 based on the empirical distributions of local group magnitudes. The resulting calibration parameters are determined offline and remain fixed during inference, while the hierarchical encoding continues to adapt to individual blocks through their local values. This design eliminates the need for per-block multi-candidate scale search while preserving the original deterministic HiF4 encoding procedure. Experiments across five synthetic and real activation distributions show that the proposed method reduces quantization MSE by 9.91%-23.50% relative to the original HiF4 scheme, including a 13.00% reduction on real Qwen3-8B activations. End-to-end evaluations across six benchmarks further show average-score improvements of 0.91 and 1.25 percentage points on Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-30B-A3B, respectively, without per-block multi-candidate search during inference.

Title: Power Side-Channel Evaluation using Multi-FPGA Systems

  • Authors: Yaswanth Tavva, Geng Luo, Trevor E. Carlson, Li-Shiuan Peh
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32652

  • Abstract: Complex out-of-order processors are difficult to evaluate for power side-channel leakage before tapeout. A single FPGA often lacks sufficient capacity, whereas conventional CAD-based analysis is too slow to support the large numbers of traces required by practical attacks. Multi-FPGA emulation provides greater capacity, but existing partitioning schemes are not security-aware and thus results in poor attack performance. This work presents Scissors, a security-aware multi-FPGA partitioning approach for pre-silicon power side-channel evaluation of complex out-of-order processors. Scissors uses side-channel vulnerability factor (SVF) analysis to identify signals correlated with the secret key. It then keeps strongly key-correlated signals confined to one FPGA partition (measured) while moving rest of the signals to the other FPGAs (support), improving the observability of the side-channel information. We evaluate Scissors through RTL simulations and physical measurements on a two-U200 FPGA testbed, using frequency-domain CPA with a Hamming-weight leakage model. RTL ablation shows that only 68-71 high-SVF signals dominate key recovery process. For a single-core processor (SingleBOOM), Scissors recovers the key with 12,000 traces, compared with 49,000 for the unpartitioned design and 43,000 for TritonPart, while improving correct-key correlation by $2.83\times$. For a quad-core processor (QuadBOOM), which exceeds the capacity of the CW340 single-FPGA platform, Scissors reduces the required traces from 111,000 to 66,000 and improves correlation by $1.48\times$ over TritonPart. Scissors also reduces QuadBOOM RTL-to-bitstream generation from 61 h 39 min to 6 h 40 min, a $9.25\times$ speedup. These results show that security-aware partitioning improves leakage observability and attack performance while substantially reducing implementation time for complex processors against the baseline method.

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32675

  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer, generative
  • Abstract: High-resolution video matting requires accurate recovery of continuous alpha values, including fine hair, soft boundaries, and motion blur, yet dense alpha supervision remains scarce. Pretrained text-to-video diffusion transformers (DiTs) offer rich spatio-temporal priors, whereas existing generative video matting approaches typically route alpha predictions through RGB-trained VAE latents, which can attenuate fine details and introduce additional decoding overhead. We present TokenMatte, which adapts a pretrained video DiT into a single-pass, auxiliary-free RGB-to-alpha predictor with a pixel-native output interface. At its core is a single shared linear readout that maps each spatio-temporal token directly to its corresponding ($t \times p \times p$) block of alpha pixels, predicting spatial details and temporal variations within the same local video block. Tiling these blocks produces the full-resolution alpha sequence without a convolutional decoder or learned upsampling. The predicted alpha is supervised directly in pixel space, while the ground-truth alpha never enters the VAE, eliminating target-side alpha encoding and output-side VAE decoding. Leveraging the pretrained video prior, TokenMatte is trained using only existing public matting datasets, without additional data curation or auxiliary inputs at inference. Compared with a matched latent-output baseline, our pixel-native interface improves matting accuracy while achieving a 1.51$\times$ inference speedup. TokenMatte establishes a new auxiliary-free state of the art on VideoMatte, YouTubeMatte, and V-HIM60.

Title: Social Bias Neurons in Multilingual Language Models: A Mechanistic Interpretability Analysis

  • Keywords: interpretability
  • Abstract: Multilingual models exhibit social bias when processing inputs pertaining to societal stereotypes. We adapt emerging mechanistic interpretability methods to pinpoint and characterize model components that encode this bias. Title: Are We Rewarding the Wrong Module? Module-Specific Policy Optimization for Multimodal Reinforcement Learning

  • Authors: HuiSeong Cho, Jun Yeong Park, Yu Rang Park
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32687
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32687
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32687]] Are We Rewarding the Wrong Module? Module-Specific Policy Optimization for Multimodal Reinforcement Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32687)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) based on Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) has been widely adopted to enhance the multimodal reasoning of multimodal large language models (MLLMs). However, existing methods share a common limitation of Module-Level Credit Entanglement, in which the vision encoder, projector, and LLM modules are jointly optimized using the same advantage signals even when perceptual correctness and final-answer correctness differ. To address this limitation, we propose Module-Specific Policy Optimization (MSPO), a framework that separates perceptual and outcome-based learning signals across model modules. Specifically, MSPO uses responses to perception checklists comprising auxiliary questions about visual information to derive an advantage for training the vision encoder. From these responses, MSPO computes the Confidence-aware Perception Reward (CaPR), using a perception sign obtained by thresholding checklist accuracy to determine reward direction and posterior confidence from a Beta distribution over latent perception accuracy to scale reward magnitude. We then derive the vision advantage through group-relative normalization of CaPR and scale it using main-task success rates. This advantage guides updates to the vision encoder, while the projector and LLM retain outcome-based GRPO updates. Extensive experiments on seven mathematical and general reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that MSPO outperforms existing RL-tuned models on average at both the 3B and 7B scales.

Title: AnyPrune: Optimization-Free Pruning for Any Feedforward 3D Gaussian Splatting Pipeline

  • Authors: Nicola Leonetti, Federico Stella, Aoxiang Fan, Saqib Javed, Pascal Fua, Luigi Di Stefano
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32735
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32735

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Feedforward 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) pipelines reconstruct a scene from images in a single forward pass, representing it as real-time-renderable 3D Gaussians. However, the resulting representations contain far more Gaussians than are needed to render the scene faithfully, especially when there are many input views. This drastically increases memory and storage requirements. Existing pruning methods either require costly re-optimization, which defeats the purpose of a single forward pass, or rely on simple strategies that do not exploit all the available information. We present AnyPrune, a transformer-based, optimization-free pruning module designed to work on top of any feedforward 3DGS pipeline. To remove unnecessary Gaussians, AnyPrune learns and exploits pipeline-specific redundancy patterns through a short single-GPU training on the frozen target feedforward pipeline. AnyPrune achieves better rendering quality than existing optimization-free pruning methods in most settings and generalizes to unseen datasets, as shown through extensive experiments across multiple feedforward pipelines, datasets, and input view counts. It prunes a 1.6M-Gaussian field in about $5\,\mathrm{s}$ on a consumer GPU, faster than every method that merges Gaussians or re-optimizes the field. It can also be combined with the pruning built into feedforward pipelines, pruning further at moderate ratios while preserving or improving rendering quality. Finally, it can even suggest a scene-specific pruning ratio that retains most of the quality.

  • Abstract: Quantization-Conditioned Backdoors (QCBs) cause large language models (LLMs) to exhibit malicious behavior after quantization while remaining benign at high precision. We isolate module-local quantization effects from upstream drift by evaluating the high-precision and quantized versions of each module on identical states from the quantized inference trajectory, and formulate QCB defense as repairing the resulting same-state operator discrepancies. Code is available at this https URL.

Title: Scaling Laws of Ensemble Uncertainty in Language Models

  • Authors: Siamak Ravanbakhsh, David Nitchi, Khang Ngo
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.NE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32801
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.32801
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.32801]] Scaling Laws of Ensemble Uncertainty in Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32801)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Many use cases for large language models (LLMs)---from AI safety to exploration and human interaction---can benefit from epistemic uncertainty estimates. We argue that existing Bayesian Deep Learning solutions, which are often restricted to a fixed model family, fail to capture the full epistemic uncertainty at practical LLM scales. This is because the error due to the bounded capacity of the model family remains invisible to disagreement among models supported by the prior. We relate this error to the well-known power-law scaling of LLM loss with model size. We then show that a second power law quantifies the shrinking disagreement within ensembles as model size increases. Its slower decay relative to the capacity law above suggests that ensembles capture a growing share of epistemic uncertainty and become more competitive as an axis for scaling, compared to a single larger model. Finally, a third power law quantifies the loss of ensemble diversity as members share a larger fraction of training before branching, and therefore identifies a further limitation, beyond the capacity error, for LoRA ensembles and optimization-based or second-order methods that approximate uncertainty around a single training solution. At a high level, these findings suggest diverse ensembles can be a promising axis, both for scaling and uncertainty quantification at larger scales.
